How ‘Godmother of K-Culture’ Miky Lee Earned the Asian Hall of Fame’s Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As CJ Group vice chair, Lee helped launch Superstar K, the MAMA Awards and KCON, making K-pop a global phenomenon.

CJ Group Vice Chair Miky Lee has received the Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement from the Asian Hall of Fame during an awards ceremony in Los Angeles, which took place on Nov. 5. The honor shows how Lee has steadily built major milestones for both Korean film and K-pop over the years.

The Asian Hall of Fame is a cross-cultural educational and research institution dedicated to recognizing Asian and Indigenous legacy, early-career achievement and interracial collaboration. In a given year, the Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement goes to only a handful of people, and recipients are chosen from figures who have built decades of global achievement and influence in fields such as culture, arts and entertainment. That sets the medal apart from the annual Class Inductees, which comprise a larger group of people.

The award carries particular weight because it recognizes Lee’s role over the past 30-odd years as a bridge between Korea and the world. Throughout her career, she has widened the base for the global spread of Korean content and exchange between cultures. Billboard Korea takes a look back at what Lee has done to help build K-pop’s global infrastructure, and at what the Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement means.

While Lee’s name is familiar in the film world, she stated during her acceptance speech in Los Angeles on Nov. 5 that music was the starting point. “And me? I simply loved music,” she said, adding that music “has been at the heart of everything we’ve built in culture.” Lee is the figure who created and developed K-pop’s anchor points, building on music platforms that span broadcasting, distribution and live performance.

The origin of the “K-survival audition,” a cradle for K-pop talent

On July 24, 2009, Mnet’s Superstar K first went on air, raising the curtain on Korea’s first modern survival audition show. It went on to produce a long list of standout stars, and countless audition programs followed the show’s model. Lee had been the one to initially suggest that Mnet look into a program of its own after she had taken note of American Idol in the U.S.

In 2016, Mnet’s Produce 101 carried the audition format over into idol survival and set off another craze. The Produce series went on to launch I.O.I, Wanna One and IZ*ONE. Boys Planet series gave rise to ZEROBASEONE and ALPHA DRIVE ONE, and I-LAND2 produced izna.

The stage didn’t stop at Korea. In Japan, the PRODUCE 101 JAPAN series formed JO1, INI and ME:I, and KO1KEYZ debuted through 2026’s PRODUCE 101 JAPAN SHINSEKAI. Today, CJ ENM’s local label business in Japan is broadening its scope beyond audition-linked groups, extending to the development of Japanese boy band DXTEEN.

The “K-audition universe” and “K-star development system” that Lee set in motion are still evolving. Next up is GIRLS PLANET 2027, a global girl group project in partnership with Universal Music Group’s REPUBLIC Collective. The show is targeting a 2027 premiere, and the two sides will collaborate on everything from production to the debut group’s global activities. Superstar K is also set to return next year with its first new season in 11 years, drawing attention from music fans.

From Korea’s first music video awards to the Dolby Theatre: the MAMA AWARDS

The MAMA AWARDS have long been one of the award shows K-pop fans both in Korea and abroad can’t help but look forward to. The show started out as the Mnet Video Music Awards, Korea’s first music video awards. The show combines Mnet’s stage production capabilities with artists’ talents to deliver visuals and performances that are talked about long afterward.

The show’s signature slogan, “Music Makes One,” has become a signal that gets K-pop fans’ hearts racing at the end of each year. Lee invoked the term in her acceptance speech, saying, “‘Music Makes One.’ That was the dream. And the fans brought it to life.” After bringing music fans from around the globe together, the MAMA AWARDS expanded to stages across Asia. In 2024, the show planted its flag for the first time at Hollywood’s Dolby Theatre, the iconic home of the Academy Awards.

Leading KCON as it grew into a global fan community

Lee spearheaded the founding and expansion of KCON, the music and culture festival that launched in California in 2012. KCON was different from the start. Alongside concerts, it offered talk sessions, workshops and artist signing events. Back in 2014 and early on in their career, BTS took the KCON LA stage, with RM later stating in an interview with Mnet that the performance there was what first earned the group a strong response in the U.S.

The road she has traveled, and the road ahead, as K-pop’s new frontier

Alongside pop legend Lionel Richie, Lee was credited as an executive producer of KPOPPED, an eight-part music series released worldwide on Apple TV+ in August 2025. Produced by CJ ENM and Eureka Productions, the show pairs Western and K-pop artists to reinterpret existing hits and perform them in front of a live audience in Seoul. ITZY teamed up with Spice Girls’ Mel B and Emma Bunton, ATEEZ with J Balvin and Kylie Minogue and BLACKSWAN with Boyz II Men for musical reinterpretations on the Seoul stage. The series opened a new horizon for K-pop and moved beyond simply exporting finished products to proving that K-pop artists are shaping the game as “equal creators” alongside the world’s top artists.

The medal was presented to Miky Lee by Lee Soo-man, key producer at A2O Entertainment. When Soo-man was inducted last year, it was Miky Lee who introduced him as the “Godfather of K-pop.” Lee Soo-man opened his remarks by saying, “I have been waiting an entire year for this moment. Tonight, it’s my turn.” His speech showed a relationship that spans more than 50 years.

“If I am the Godfather of K-pop, then Miky Lee is the Godmother of K-culture,” Lee Soo-man said, praising her contributions to culture.

Their speeches doubled as a chronicle of Korean entertainment. Miky Lee looked back on the 1995 investment in DreamWorks and said, “We wanted to help lay the foundation for Korea’s entertainment industry.” She continued, “In the past, nearly 85% of songs on Korean TV were American pop songs, but by the 2000s, it was completely the other way around.” She traced the journey through Mnet, MAMA and KCON, and closed her acceptance by saying, “Now, it’s our turn to believe in the next generation.” The question that now lies is just how far the next generation will carry the K-pop frontier she has expanded.

MotoGP U.S. Rights Head to ESPN in Deal With Liberty Media

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The motorcycle racing circuit, a sister brand top Formula 1, was most recently with Fox Sports.

ESPN is back in business with Liberty Media.

The sports media juggernaut has inked a deal to broadcast MotoGP, the motorcycle racing circuit owned by Liberty, beginning in 2027.

It’s a two-year deal that will cover both the U.S. and Latin America, where ESPN already had MotoGP rights. MotoGP’s most recent U.S. rights were with Fox Sports, and they had previously been with Warner Bros. Discovery and NBC Sports.

ESPN say that the races will air on all of ESPN’s platforms, as well as on Disney+.

Liberty Media, which is led by Derek Chang, owns both MotoGP and Formula 1, which left ESPN this year to move its U.S. rights to Apple TV. Liberty bought MotoGP in 2024 at a $4.5 billion valuation with a goal of building it into a worldwide brand as it has done with F1. The U.S. market, for obvious reasons, is a top priority, so ESPN’s reach is seen as a boost to that effort.

“This agreement with ESPN marks an important milestone in MotoGP’s growth strategy across the Americas,” said Carlos Ezpeleta, deputy CEO of MotoGP. “ESPN’s position as one of the world’s leading sports broadcasters, combined with its strong connection with audiences across the United States and Latin America, makes it the ideal platform for us to continue expanding MotoGP’s reach and bring the sport closer to millions of people. This is an exciting opportunity to introduce MotoGP to new audiences while continuing to serve our passionate existing fan base through one of the most influential sports media brands in the world.”

“ESPN and MotoGP have a strong relationship that dates back nearly 25 years, when we began bringing the championship to fans across Latin America,” added Freddy Rolón, head of global sports & talent office for ESPN. “This is a great example of how relationships that begin in one region of our international business can grow across ESPN’s global portfolio, creating new opportunities to connect different audiences with world-class sporting events.”

Democrats want to stop Trump's arch, golf course and more. What are their options?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Trump signed the final steel beam at the White House ballroom the day after the midterm elections. Andrew Harnik/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump's efforts to remake Washington, D.C., have dominated his second term, even after his party's resounding midterm defeat.

He started his election week at the scandal-plagued Reflecting Pool and ended it with an order declaring his gilded Oval Office a national monument to "make it impossible for anybody ever to destroy this incredible space."

That proclamation, like any executive order, could be undone by a future president. But many of Trump's construction projects are changing D.C. in more permanent ways.

His administration has already downed hundreds of trees at East Potomac Park and demolished the East Wing of the White House to make space for a ballroom — which he called "the safest place in Washington" as he showed it to reporters the day after the midterms.

Trump has taken a uniquely unilateral approach to construction, packing regulatory commissions with his allies and bypassing Congress entirely. Most of his projects — from the triumphal arch to the Kennedy Center — are now playing out in court.

Critics accuse the Trump administration of skirting federal laws and misleading judges in its effort to progress as quickly as possible before the end of his term.

Democrats have vowed to use their soon-to-be congressional majority to push back on what they describe as Trump's "vanity projects."

When asked about that, White House spokesperson Davis Ingle told NPR in an email that "no other president has done more to beautify our Nation's interior than President Trump." He cited park cleanups, the Reflecting Pool and the White House helipad and ballroom as examples, and said Democrats "should work with President Trump to make our nation's capital beautiful again."

Rep. Jared Huffman, center, poses with activists protesting Trump's triumphal arch at Memorial Circle in May. He will soon become the chair of the committee with legislative and oversight authority over public lands. Andrew Harnik/Getty Imags hide caption

Rep. Jared Huffman, D.-Calif., told NPR in an interview that the Trump administration has "played this game of Catch Me If You Can, where they roll ahead with the bulldozers and the tree cutters … and they wait for courts to shut them down." He says a Democrat-led Congress will fight back.

"There's no way that we could look away while these sacred, iconic places are being permanently changed in ways that violate the law and could have really terrible lasting impacts," said Huffman, the top Democrat on the House Natural Resources Committee, which has a legislative purview over public lands and national parks.

Huffman says blocking Trump's construction projects — and holding people accountable for potential wrongdoing — will be a top priority when he leads the committee. Earlier this week, he sent letters ordering dozens of administration officials and private companies to preserve their records for a future investigation.

"Whether it is the arch or … or the East Potomac golf course or any number of other examples around the United States capital, that is federal land, and we have a direct statutory responsibility to either authorize those changes or to stop them if they're proceeding without the proper authorization," Huffman said.

What would that actually look like? Democrats have many tools at their disposal, from legislation to lawsuits. But experts say only two are likely to succeed: funding and oversight.

Trump made a final pre-Midterm Day pitch to voters from the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, whose renovations took months longer than anticipated due to algae blooms and peeling paint. Saul Loeb/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Congress could — at least in theory — pass laws to block specific projects from moving ahead. Some Democratic lawmakers have already introduced bills to protect the Kennedy Center from demolition and prohibit the construction of an arch at Memorial Circle.

But Trump has promised to "veto everything," and Democrats won't have a big enough majority to override him on their own.

"Legislation in principle is the cleanest way of doing it, but it's also not achievable," said David Super, who teaches administrative and constitutional law at Georgetown. "So what they're likely to do is cut off funds."

The Constitution says that the federal government may spend money only if is appropriated by Congress. But Trump has neither sought nor received congressional authorization for projects like the arch and the White House ballroom — he maintains he doesn't need it.

Rep. Chellie Pingree of Maine is one of many who disagree. She's the top Democrat on the House committee that oversees funding for the Interior Department.

"Certainly Congress should have been involved, because we have the power of the purse," Pingree told NPR. "That is our ultimate tool. And Republicans have refused to use that. But we can use that in very many ways to block him or to use it for leverage."

Pingree says a Democratic majority will be able to write appropriations bills with clear language from the outset about where money can and can't be spent — and potentially take the president to court if he doesn't follow it.

Super expects Congress would prevail in such a challenge, because it has clear-cut authority over federal funds.

For example, he noted that some of Trump's D.C.-area beautification projects have been funded at least in part by diverted national park entrance fees. Congress could "cap how much money can be used in the greater Washington area, and force the money to be used in the Everglades and Yosemite and Great Smoky Mountains," he said.

Congress will have to make some appropriations decisions in the coming weeks, since it needs to pass a short-term funding bill by Dec. 11 to avoid a government shutdown. Democrats could use that opportunity to try to limit funding for at least one of Trump's construction projects.

Huffman has proposed language that would prevent Trump from using federal funds for the East Potomac golf course renovations, limiting them instead to repairs of the park's crumbling seawall. Pingree said she will join him in fighting to negotiate that into next month's bill.

On one October Saturday, the Trump administration cut down some 430 trees at East Potomac Park, where the president wants to develop a municipal golf course into a championship-level course. Aaron Schwartz/Getty Images hide caption

"Whether or not the president will sign that with that in there, I don't know yet," she said. "But certainly those are the most … immediate crisis points … People want the president to stop cutting down the trees on the golf course and stop trying to take that over, and they want to make sure that no shovel goes into the ground on building the arch."

Earlier this year Pingree introduced an amendment that would have outright blocked construction of the arch; it failed along party lines. She wonders whether any Republicans might vote differently after the midterms, given the message Americans sent at the polls.

"They want the president to be focused on the issues they're dealing with: the cost of health care, the cost of food, the cost of gasoline," she said. "And if every time the president gives a speech, he's either standing in front of the Reflecting Pool, which he spent millions of dollars on, or showing you the new marble choices he's putting into the ballroom, it just makes a lot of people mad."

Pingree said she's been surprised to hear how strongly people feel about these projects – even people well outside D.C. And she agrees with them that potential corruption and misuse of taxpayer dollars are worth fighting: "After the first of the year, we will use every tool in our toolbox."

Lawmakers and experts acknowledge that not every project is reversible, or potentially even worth the fight. Huffman suggested he will focus on bigger projects, and ones that are moving too fast for courts to adequately rein in.

"Are we going to throw all of our efforts at stopping the gilding of every statue in Washington, D.C.? Probably not," said Huffman. "But these are iconic places that we're talking about, with permanent damage that we need to engage on to try to prevent."

Even so, Democrats could decide that certain construction projects are "the price they pay for other priorities," said Claire Leavitt, a professor of government at Smith College. Voters made clear they want lawmakers to focus on ending the Iran war and making life more affordable, big asks that may take a lot of political capital.

"And that's not corruption or Democrats not doing their job," Leavitt said. "It's just the natural trade-offs of dealing with who you're dealing with, and understanding that what matters to Trump more than anything seems to be building the arch, building the ballroom … remaking the Kennedy Center."

Whether or not these projects move forward, Congress can provide a fuller public accounting of how they came to be — in the hopes of preventing more in the future.

"They can gavel in hearings. They have agenda-setting power," Leavitt said. "They determine what the public hearing roster is. They decide which witnesses to invite. And that means they have a unique opportunity to draw negative public attention to the Trump administration."

Work on the White House ballroom is moving quickly. Andrew Harnik/Getty Images hide caption

That may be easier said than done, especially with an administration that has pointedly refused to comply with congressional oversight and is now warning Democrats that their future investigations could constitute criminality. Huffman was coy when asked how he is preparing to handle such challenges.

"We are going to have to have a strategy and a plan to do these things that prior Congresses have never imagined they would have to do," he said.

One investigative priority, lawmakers say, will be following the money — especially when it comes to donations for the ballroom and other projects. Leavitt says that focus has an added benefit: Private companies and individuals are likely more willing to testify than a member of the administration.

Another example: Congress could subpoena for information about the decision-making processes of the Kennedy Center board and the physical state of the building, which trustees say must be closed for two years of repairs. And it can put that information in the Congressional — and therefore historical — Record.

"Both the current public can make its judgments, and history can make its judgments," Leavitt said.

Leavitt said oversight can often lead to policy change in the longer term. For example: The committee investigating the Jan. 6 insurrection didn't stop Trump from eventually pardoning Capitol rioters, but it did lead Congress to pass an electoral reform act.

"The frustrating part about congressional oversight is Congress can draw attention to a problem, and they can demand accountability for something that's already been done," Leavitt said. "But it's very hard for Congress to stop something from happening that the president is determined to do."

Super agrees that Democrats are playing a long game. He expects to see more bipartisan support after Trump leaves office for preventing future presidents from following his playbook.

"The process in the past has been built on trust, and President Trump certainly broke that trust in numerous ways," he said. "Laws can be written so that we don't depend on trust, we depend on law. And I'm guessing we're going to do that."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

GTA 6 Nearly Hit With Ban Over ‘Frequent Male Genital Exposure’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6 mere days away, we already know to expect a whole lot of mature subject matter. Official promotional materials like the Netflix Extended Look have prominently featured strip clubs, drug deals gone wrong, and chaotic vehicular shootouts. A new report out of South Korea from Inven Global claims members of the Game Content Rating Board (GCRB) nearly refused to issue a rating for GTA 6, which would have effectively banned the game there. The main sticking point? An excessive amount of "out of context genital nudity."

According to the report, the GCRB held three separate review sessions to determine its rating for GTA 6. The first review took place on Sept. 10, before committee members had access to a playable build. That ended in a tie, with three voting in favor of refusal and three in favor of an Adults Only rating. "I do not believe genital exposure to this extent falls within acceptable limits under the current standard," said one of the members who refused a rating. It's possible that this assessment was shaped by the massive GTA 6 gameplay leaks that took place over the summer, which showed Jason interacting with unclothed patrons at a nudist resort and nonchalantly cruising around the streets of Miami with his dong hanging out.

The GCRB met again on Oct. 2, but ultimately opted to postpone its decision a second time, having obtained a copy of the game just a few days earlier. "Further inspection is necessary, but if a rating is actually granted, we should take this opportunity to review changing the regulation that treats simple genital exposure as grounds for refusal," said one of the committee members.

During the final review session on Oct. 16, the committee voted eight to one in favor of issuing an Adults Only (19+) rating. "It appears that they intentionally covered genital exposure in explicitly sexual scenes, while allowing exposure in background settings," explained one voter from the majority. The lone dissenter expressed concerns that this would mean open season on background penises in future games. "Suppose we consider genre characteristics to permit background genital exposure in adventures or RPGs while remaining conservative only with suggestive games," they said. "I am concerned that background genital exposure will frequently appear in future RPGs as a result." Don't get any ideas, Square Enix!

While it is pretty funny to imagine people having high-stakes debates about how much dong is too much dong for a video game, this conflict indirectly tells us some interesting things about GTA 6. We know there will "explicitly sexual scenes" in the game, but you won't see anyone's business district during those moments. In other words, Rockstar isn't bringing the infamous Hot Coffee sex minigame from GTA: San Andreas into 2026. That's probably for the best.

GTA 6 has received strict adult age classifications in virtually every market. It's got an M (17+) rating in North America, and 18+ ratings in Japan, Europe, the UK, Australia, New Zealand, Hong Kong, India, Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, South Africa, Taiwan, Thailand, and Brazil. The game has a 21+ rating in Saudi Arabia and the UAE. GTA 6 will be released on Nov. 19 for PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X.

XRP Funding Rate Hits One-Year High, ETFs Still Bleed

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » XRP Funding Rate Hits One-Year High, ETFs Still Bleed

XRP’s hurdles on the ETF markets conflict with the generally-bullish sentiment on Perpetuals. Who’s right?

XRP’s Derivatives crypto traders have been on the look-out for a bear trap. The latest Binance Funding Rate data showcases a scenario where the 30-day cumulative rate reached 0.18%, the biggest point in more than a year.

CryptoQuant’s analyst Dark Frost has dug into the historical data behind the shift, witnessing a similar structure just two months before XRP hit $3.55 back in 2025. The rolling monthly figure is particularly important to swing traders looking for the price bottom, below all 4 moving averages.

Traders have taken a keen interest in the $0.98 – $1 liquidity pocket, the same that served XRP as a trampoline to $1.67 back in August, 2026. With the positioning on Binance’s Derivatives now on the bullish side, the sentiment needs confirmation from whales buying on Spot.

In regards to the XRP coin funding rate hike on Binance, that kind of shift often precedes a price trend reversal. “Extreme readings in either direction can signal that traders are crowding into the same side of the market”, says Dark Frost on X. The 30-day sum hints at a few scenarios.

🗞️ XRP funding rates on Binance hit a 1Y high while spot ETFs see $10.4M in net outflowsFunding rates for ripple:native perpetual futures on Binance have reached their highest level in more than a year. The last time rates were this high was in October 2025, a few months after… pic.twitter.com/ZZte6Vfud4

Over the period shown, peaks in bullish sentiment coincided with XRP price tops, while the most negative funding readings occurred near market bottoms. XRP has already entered a 30% price correction from the cycle peak, similarly to when XRP rebounded 53% after falling to $0.70.

For CryptoQuant’s experts, this resembles strong conviction. “XRP’s 30% correction illustrates the risk of maintaining a bullish bias as prices fall” said the analyst. However, that comes with a price on its own. The data from Futures markets tells a story of XRP bulls getting beat down.

The liquidation data has been lob-sided, with XRP’s bulls incurring a $11.01 million deficit in the 24-hour time-frame, says CoinGlass. The ratios from all five top-traded XRP coin venues are displaying long versus short figures drastically below 1, meaning a bearish crowd sentiment.

On Wall Street, the bearish sentiment is felt even harder. Spot XRP ETFs recorded one of their worst sessions of the year, with daily net outflows hitting 9.37 million XRP, equal to $10.4 million. The altcoin’s ETF items slimmed by $18 million in two days, leaving bears hungry for more.

‘Ebenezer’ Review: Johnny Depp Blissfully Goes All Scrooge In Ti West’s New Horror-Flavored Take On Dickens Christmas Classic

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There have been countless iterations on the Charles Dickens yuletide classic, “A Christmas Carol” which was first birthed to the world as a novella in 1843, but has been a favorite of Hollywood for nearly a century. Actors have warmed to the bah humbug of it all in various takes beginning with Reginald Owen in 1938’s A Christmas Carol to perhaps still the best, Alastair Sim in the 1951 version. George C. Scott and Patrick Stewart did well-regarded TV movies. Bill Murray did a contemporary comedic version with Scrooged. Albert Finney turned it all into a big screen musical in 1970. Even Kermit and Miss Piggy jumped on board with The Muppet Christmas Carol, and no less than Mr. Magoo in the evergreen (and personal favorite) Mr. Magoo’s Christmas Carol. In 2017 Christopher Plummer even took a whirl with Scrooge in the clever and wonderful biopic, The Man Who Invented Christmas which was all about Dickens writing the time honored tale. Related Stories News Daisy Ridley, Darren Criss, Travis Fimmel & Judy Greer Join Catherine Zeta-Jones In Tate Taylor's Marriage Counselor Thriller 'Cupid'; Filming Underway -- AFM News Johnny Depp & Ti West Land In A Chilly London For 'Ebenezer' Global Premiere

It seems inevitable though that modern day master of all disguises, Johnny Depp would get a turn at bat, and in the latest version, Ebenezer, he has done just that. To say it is a perfect fit for Depp would be an understatement as he finds his own unique way into the role, but in a bit of a twist manages to unearth more humanity in Ebenezer Scrooge than previous versions that saved all that for the inevitable happy ending. Here, insufferable as he can be, this Scrooge allows us to have more empathy for the man as his own past is revealed as the source of his discontent. The other wrinkle with this version is brought by its director, Ti West, previously known as a horrormeister with the trilogy of X, Pearl, and MaXXXine, but it turns out he is a lifelong fan of the Dickens tale and found it ripe for more emphasis on its horror aspects built in with the infamous ghosts so crucial to the story. After all, the original title of the novella was “A Christmas Carol. In Prose. Being A Ghost Story At Christmas”. Paramount’s campaign for the movie also leans into this with the ad line: “If You Think You Know His Story, Think Again”. Fear not though, we do know the story and this is still all very PG13 and not as bloodletting as the filmmaker’s past filmography. West , and his screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern, have too much reverance for what Dickens hath wrought to mess around with the bones of the tale we know all too well. Paramount’s marketing department wants us to believe this is all new but it really isn’t for the most part, just an excuse for Depp to play in the sandbox. Watch on Deadline

Ebenezer, with fine period production design by Eve Stewart and darkly lit cinematography from Checco Varese, perfectly sets the tone as we meet Ebenezer Scrooge and his even more greedy business partner Jacob Marley (an ideal Ian McKellen), just before Marley succumbs to a heart attack. It is Christmas Eve and neither owner was all too swell to loyal but beaten-down employee Bob Cratchit (an effective Rupert Grint) who is anxious to spend time with his family including wife Clara (Daisy Ridley) and young son Tiny Tim (Freddie Lee Grey). There is also a visit from Christmas-loving nephew Fred (Sam Claflin) who can’t understand his uncle’s disdain for the holiday and what it really means.

As Scrooge retires for the night, the fireworks begin, first with a view from down under from the late not-so-great Marley with a foreboding warning. Then we have a visit from the Ghost Of Christmas Past (a spirited Andrea Riseborough) who takes him back (Arthur Conti is the younger Ebenezer) to his roots growing up, to his own family life, and most notably to his one and only true love Belle (Ellie Bamber) in a relationship doomed to failure as his early inclination for money over love seems to take hold. Here we see the human being, flawed but recognizable, taking his first wrong turn. Cue the Ghost Of Christmas Present (a dynamic turn from Severance Emmy winner Tramell Tillman) who really turns on the gas to show Scrooge a miserable world, including for the Cratchit clan involving death and despair. West finds his deepest dive into the tropes of horror with this sequence, about as dark as any version of A Christmas Carol can get. And then it gets even darker with the Ghost Of Christmas Future (creature actor Robert Strange) who is so imposing he needs to say nothing, just show what will become of Ebenezer – and it is not pretty.

It is no spoiler to reveal how it all works out and Depp clearly is having a blast playing it to the hilt. Fortunately the audience will walk out with their Christmas spirit (if not the spirits) intact.

I am not sure the world needed yet another dance with Dickens and Scrooge. As mentioned at the top of this review there is plenty to savor from the cinema of Christmases Past that hold up quite nicely to revisit year after year after year, but I have to say it is satisfying to see Depp give it a go. After a very rough patch in his life and career, just like Ebenezer Scrooge, this gifted actor is seeing the light again.

Title: EbenezerDistributor: ParamountRelease Date: November 13, 2026Director: Ti WestScreenwriter: Nathaniel HalpernCast: Johnny Depp, Rupert Grint, Daisy Ridley, Andrea Riseborough, Sam Claflin, Ian McKellen, Tramell Tillman, Robert Strange, Ellie Bamber, Arthur Conti, Freddie Lee GreyRating: PG13Running Time: 1 hr 56 mins

Stayd Collective: Cleaning Operations Coordinator

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Schedule: Monday–Friday, 8:00am–5:00pm ET | Occasional weekend coverage as needed

Compensation: USD $1,200–$1,500 / month depending on experience.

About the brandHaven manages cabins and mountain homes in the Great Smoky Mountains and around Knoxville, Tennessee. Steep driveways, hot tubs, wood stoves, and a season that swings from quiet weeks to a full house every weekend. It is a market where property care is guest experience: a cold hot tub is a ruined anniversary, and a turnover that runs late is a family waiting in the driveway.

The roleYou are the person who makes sure every cabin is guest-ready before the next family arrives. You are the main communication point between Haven operations and our Tendwell cleaning teams. Your day starts with the departures, arrivals and cleaning schedule, and from there you follow up with cleaners on progress and ETAs, clear whatever is blocking a turn, coordinate re-cleans and supply fixes, and confirm each home is ready before a same-day check-in.

When a guest checks out late, you work out what it does to the cleaning schedule and tell operations what timing is realistic. It is a role for someone who is highly responsive and calm under same-day pressure. Asking for an update is not the same as getting the home ready. If a turn is yours, you own it until it is confirmed complete.

Where you sitYou sit in Housekeeping & Field Ops and work every day with Tendwell cleaners and Haven’s operations team. This is not a role where you are handed a queue and left alone.

This role is remote and based in LATAM, covering U.S. Eastern Time working hours. Because Stayd runs shared systems across every brand, a process you improve here tends to end up running in six other markets, which is the closest thing we have to a promotion path.

'Yes': SBI CEO Yoshitaka Kitao Answers XRP Holder Doubts in One Word

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Yoshitaka Kitao, head of Japanese financial group SBI Holdings, answered "YES" when asked point-blank whether he still trusts XRP.

The question came from an X user. The user wrote that the past few weeks had been "truly exhausting" for XRP holders, and said that token holders feel that Ripple has left them behind.

Kitao answered in English. Most of his recent posts on X are in Japanese, and the rest of them are reposts of other accounts.

Kitao previously worked as an executive at Nomura Securities and later served as CFO of SoftBank. In 1999, he founded SoftBank Investment, which later became SBI Holdings. Today, SBI includes banks, brokerage, and insurance businesses, as well as crypto services.

The partnership began in 2016. SBI joined Ripple's Series B round and launched a joint venture with Ripple, SBI Ripple Asia. SBI owns about 60% of the venture, and Ripple owns the remaining 40%.

The same year, SBI Ripple Asia set up a consortium of Japanese banks to test Ripple's technology for payments, and the consortium later grew to more than 60 banks.

In 2018, SBI launched the interbank app MoneyTap. The same year, SBI's crypto exchange, now called SBI VC Trade, started operations with XRP trading. In 2019, Kitao joined Ripple's board of directors.

From 2020, SBI began handing the token out to shareholders as bonuses, and the payment service SBI Remit launched remittances using it with on-demand liquidity (ODL), including in the major Japan-Philippines corridor.

SBI kept working with Ripple during the SEC lawsuit against the company, which lasted from December 2020 until 2025.

Back on February 15, 2026, in rare long-form tweets in English, Kitao denied rumors that SBI holds $10 billion worth of XRP: "Not $10 billion in XRP, but around 9% of Ripple Labs. So our hidden asset could be much bigger." By his account, the combined valuation of Ripple Labs and its ecosystem is "enormous," and SBI owns more than 9% of all of it.

Kitao also talked about XRP at SBI's quarterly briefing on November 2, 2026. He said that investors buy Bitcoin mostly to hold it, and that the value of XRP depends on its use in payments and business ecosystems.

In August 2025, SBI VC Trade and Ripple agreed to bring the RLUSD stablecoin to Japan. In October 2025, SBI also invested $200 million in Evernorth, a company that holds XRP in its treasury. By now, SBI VC Trade has more than two million accounts.

CJ McCollum joins NBA on Prime crew, discusses role on Hawks, Damian Lillard's comeback

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

CJ McCollum hit the game-winning shot in the Atlanta Hawks' Game 3 victory over the New York Knicks in the first round of last year's playoffs.

Not only does he remember that shot, but he remembers enjoying being a guest on Prime Video's "NBA Nightcap" postgame show where he appeared in his pajamas and the hosts joked he put the Knicks "to sleep." They all laughed about his role of villain as his son popped in to say hello.

The party doesn't have to stop there as NBA on Prime announced on Thursday, Nov. 12 that the 14-year veteran guard will be a player commentator for several programs this season starting with "NBA Nightcap" on Friday, Nov. 13.

"The conversation and team analysis that night was really fun," he told USA TODAY Sports of the postgame memories, "and I thought the chemistry was there, and I felt like it made a lot of sense (for me to join the Prime team). And I have a great appreciation for what NBA on Prime is doing, what the crew is doing, and how they educate and share authentic analysis."

McCollum, 35, majored in journalism at Lehigh University and has experience hosting his own podcast with "The CJ McCollum Show" and "Pull Up with CJ McCollum."

Among Prime's All-Star team of talent that he is joining is a mix of longtime broadcasters, including Kevin Harlan, and basketball greats like Udonis Haslem, Dirk Nowitzki, Steve Nash and Candace Parker. McCollum said the team is "a really cool atmosphere, really great environment of people that I respect and admire."

"They're very honest. They watch the game. They're students of the game. They're people who appreciate the game and they do a great job of describing why certain things are happening," he continued. "... When you have that group of professionals who have played the game, have talked the game, have lived the game, have been around it and seen it for so long, you get various impactful moments where viewers are able to really get a glimpse of what's actually happening, why it's happening, in an insightful way that they can digest."

McCollum appreciates the craft of journalism and how showing the human element of any story brings people together. As journalism continues to face industry challenges, the 2016 Most Improved Player has hope.

"Storytelling is vital. I think it makes the world a better place," he said. "Being able to hear people's stories, being able to see people's journeys not just in the sports world but in life, I think is something that we all can appreciate.

"And journalism isn't dying. It's just evolving. But I think the people who move with integrity, who do things the right way and really appreciate the craft are continuing to thrive for sure."

McCollum shared with USA TODAY Sports some thoughts about various NBA topics as a sneak peek of what fans can expect from him as a guest commentator on Prime Video.

McCollum re-signed with the Hawks this summer after joining the team from the Washington Wizards last season in the Trae Young trade. He remains a veteran leader even as he is coming off the bench and reached the 17,000 points mark when he scored 21 in Atlanta's loss on Monday, Nov. 9 to the Los Angeles Lakers.

"It means a lot," he said of reaching the scoring mark. "It's a sign of longevity, being able to be around for a long time and play at a high level. It's a sign of hard work and appreciation for the sport. But it's also a sign of playing with good teammates, good coaching staff, people that have been able to help me get the best out of my talent and utilize a lot of the resources that we have around us and turn that into some success on the court."

Jalen Johnson is a rising star for Atlanta who helped the Hawks get to a four-game winning streak before the loss. On Saturday, Nov. 7, the 24-year-old scored 30 points in his return from an ankle injury to help with a comeback win over the Brooklyn Nets.

"Obviously, the scoring speaks for itself," McCollum said. "His vision, I think, could be one of his best traits. He's a great passer, leading transition, elite on the break, great paint finisher, and a guy who rebounds at a high level as well. And I think he's a guy that really appreciates the game, is a student of the game, studies it, lives it, and I think the sky is the limit for him."

Atlanta was on full display last month when the Atlanta Dream made it to the WNBA Finals. Angel Reese and company lost the championship to the Golden State Valkyries, but made headlines with performances from Quavo, Big Boi and a heartwarming national anthem from T.I.'s daughter, Heiress Harris.

McCollum pointed out that, at the time of the interview, the Usher and Chris Brown tour was in town and the Atlanta Falcons were in the middle of the NFL season as extra reason that the city was the place to be.

"The energy is always high in Atlanta," he said. "... Atlanta's in a really great place. It's obviously a place of its own, where you have food, you have culture, you have people from different walks of life."

Something this season that NBA fans were anticipating was the return of Damian Lillard. McCollum was teammates with the nine-time All-Star on the Portland Trail Blazers, where they both started their careers. McCollum was there for eight seasons before being traded to the New Orleans Pelicans midway though the 2021-22 season.

Lillard tore his Achilles tendon in the 2025 playoffs with the Milwaukee Bucks and missed the entirety of last season after going back to Portland. In his comeback this fall, he's averaging 20 points per game, had two 25-point efforts and his jumpshot still looks like water.

"I think there was uncertainty from the outside. But for people that knew Dame, know his work ethic, his diligence, his approach to things, we knew he was going to have this type of comeback," he said. "He's worked extremely hard, and I'm thankful and happy to be his friend and have played with him for so long. And to see him be able to overcome something like this is not surprising at all.

"I've seen how he's worked. (We've) been in contact with each other consistently. That's my guy, spent some time together in Oregon when I was out there in the summer, and this is a guy who takes his craft very seriously. He's a pro's pro, takes care of his body, takes care of his mind, and he's in a great space being home again and being back in Oregon, where it all started, that I think is a match made in heaven for him, and I'm happy to see him thriving."

Another hot topic of conversation in the NBA is the second apron, which was established in the 2023 collective bargaining agreement when McCollum was president of the NBPA. The rule creates what can be compared to a hard salary cap that has firm penalties if exceeded.

"The conception of this years ago was more about creating parity," McCollum said, "owners, teams, the union coming to an agreement that there needed to be some changes within the systems of how to strengthen some of the lower tier markets."

The second apron has been criticized for doing too much to squash superteams and breaking up championship rosters. Bruce Brown specifically said that he had trouble in free agency and teams were using the second apron as a reason not to sign him. Karl-Anthony Towns, who won the NBA Finals with the Knicks last season, also said he's had difficulties getting a contract he thinks he's worthy of.

McCollum said that only three years into the new CBA, it's too early to determine if a rule like the second apron is working.

"When you're creating something, you don't necessarily know how it's going to turn out, obviously," he continued. "And as you kind of go through something, you kind of figure out the amendments that need to take place, make sure that teams aren't being punished for drafting well.

"... We're only a few years into it. There's more things that need to be evaluated. It's hard to project what types of impacts something like an apron is going to have on the actual management and construction of a roster and how that affects contracts in the long run. But I think you need more data, more information to truly judge whether or not you're heading in the right direction."

Houston Rockets guard Fred VanVleet became NBPA president in 2025 and McCollum applauded his successor's handling of the role.

"He's doing a phenomenal job," he said. "A guy who's very mature, has continued to figure out ways to help build the business of basketball, collaborating with the NBA from a partnership standpoint. I think he's doing an excellent job. I think his leadership, obviously, on the basketball court, but his leadership off the court is something that people respect, his peers respect, and excited to see what he continues to do for not only our players but also for the league."

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: CJ McCollum joins NBA on Prime crew, discusses role on Hawks, Damian Lillard's comeback

9 foods in the Mediterranean diet and the trades behind them, per Mayo Clinic

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Mayo Clinic's Mediterranean diet advice works as a set of trades. See what 9 foods replace and how firm the evidence behind each one is.

Most diets are known by what they forbid. The Mediterranean diet makes more sense through what people who follow it eat at almost every meal and what they save for a few nights a week. Produce, whole grains and olive oil are everyday foods in this way of eating, while fish and eggs are among the foods kept for only a few meals each week. How often a food appears is a sign of how much it matters to the pattern, and the pattern sets proportions across a week rather than a fixed menu. That schedule turns most food choices into trades. When a reader moves one food into a daily slot, another food has to leave the plate, and the health case for the change often depends on which food left.

The diet earned its reputation for heart health from people who ate this way as a whole culture, not from people who added one food to an otherwise unchanged diet. The research behind it comes mostly from Mediterranean populations, according to StatPearls. That evidence describes a whole way of eating more than a set of portions. For that reason, health advice on the diet is about starting a pattern of eating, not taking a dose of any single food, and the benefit belongs to the whole pattern before it belongs to any one item. A reader can turn that into a working rule: follow the shape of the diet faithfully, and treat any single serving number as a target to aim near rather than a line that guarantees a result.

Mayo Clinic lays out practical steps for starting a heart-healthy Mediterranean eating pattern, covering foods from seafood and nuts to eggs and herbs and spices. Each of those steps comes down to a trade, and the evidence behind the trades is not equally strong. Some swaps have direct proof behind them. Others are cautious limits set where scientists still disagree. A reader who knows which is which can decide where to be strict and where to be flexible. The slides that follow take each food in turn and look at what it replaces, how strong its case is and how closely a reader needs to follow the amount attached to it.

The vegetable target looks very different on the plate depending on how the vegetables are prepared. One serving of leafy greens is two cups, while one serving of cooked vegetables is half a cup. The same four servings can be a heaped salad bowl or a modest side dish. A reader who struggles to finish large salads can reach the target with smaller portions of cooked vegetables.

Eating produce every day is common ground, but the exact counts are less fixed than the habit. Sharon F. Daley of Cape Cod Hospital in Hyannis, Mass., and Melissa R. Hinson of Conemaugh Memorial describe a guideline framework in StatPearls that differs from Mayo Clinic's daily serving counts for vegetables and fruit. A reader can hold firm to eating produce every day and to fruit in place of dessert, and let the balance between fruit and vegetables follow taste, the season and the budget.

Whole grains belong on the plate every day because their benefit is broad rather than dramatic. Mayo Clinic advises eating them each day, and the research points the same way. A European Heart Journal analysis of over 6,500 adults links whole grains to possible improvement in a group of heart risk factors rather than in just one, according to Harvard Health. Small gains across several risk factors can add up to more than a large change in one. People get that kind of modest benefit only by repeating the habit, so whole grains fit an everyday meal better than an occasional one.

The case for whole grains has limits. The research tracks risk factors, not heart attacks or strokes. It supports whole grains as a sensible staple without showing that they prevent heart disease, which makes them a well-founded bet rather than a settled cure. Mayo Clinic measures whole grains by the ounce, and 1 ounce is about one slice of bread. A single slice of whole-wheat toast at breakfast therefore counts as only one serving. A reader who stops there keeps the daily habit in name more than on the plate.

Eating more than a slice or two is easier when bread is not the only source. Mayo Clinic also names bulgur, barley and farro, which a cook can use as the base of a salad, a grain bowl or a side dish. With those grains on hand, a large daily helping becomes part of an ordinary meal rather than a chore.

Make whole grains the base of the plate at every meal, and count each slice of bread as just one ounce toward the day.

Cooks get the heart benefit of olive oil only when they use it in place of butter. The oil has a reputation as a health food in its own right, but Mayo Clinic includes unsaturated plant fats in the diet because people who use them instead of butter-type fats cut their heart disease risk and their LDL cholesterol. The gain depends on the fat that leaves the pan more than on the oil that goes in. A cook who adds olive oil to salads but still cooks with butter has added a food without making the trade.

The proven payoff of the swap is narrow, and it centers on bad cholesterol. Fats from plants that are unsaturated appear to bring down a person's LDL cholesterol along with total cholesterol. Replacing butter with plant oils and spreads had no effect on HDL cholesterol or triglycerides, according to USDA NESR. The improvement is real, but it touches one part of the blood lipid picture rather than all of it.

The heart-disease benefit rests on softer ground. "Replacing saturated fats with unsaturated fats may help lower the risk of heart disease," Mayo Clinic staff wrote. Research on trading butter for plant oil does not settle the question, USDA NESR reports, and the studies differ on whether the swap raises or lowers cardiovascular risk and by how much. People can count on the swap to improve their cholesterol and should treat any heart protection beyond that as a likely bonus rather than a promise.

Some plant fats belong on the butter side of the trade. Blood pressure stayed the same when palm oil, coconut oil or cocoa butter gave way to unsaturated oils, according to USDA NESR. These tropical fats still count among the ones to swap out, though a reader should not expect lower blood pressure from the change. At the store, the useful test is whether a fat is unsaturated, as olive oil is, not whether it comes from a plant.

Make liquid plant oil the everyday cooking fat in place of butter, and count it as a cholesterol step rather than a cure for heart disease.

Fish is one of the proteins the diet saves for a few nights a week, and part of its value lies in the meat it replaces on those nights. The health case for the fish itself is firmer for some benefits than for others. Mayo Clinic says the omega-3 fats in seafood may curb inflammation and stroke risk, and the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements reports that intake of EPA and DHA, two omega-3 fats, lowers triglyceride levels. The triglyceride effect is stated as established, while the stroke and inflammation benefits that justify eating fish are worded only as possibilities. Mayo Clinic links the omega-3 fats to fatty fish, yet no recommended amounts have been established for EPA and DHA. Omega-3 is a plausible reason to eat fish, not a measured prescription, so the safer bet is the fish itself, eaten as a meal.

Mayo Clinic asks for more fish than the heart association does, setting a target of 2 to 3 seafood meals a week. The American Heart Association calls for 1 to 2 servings weekly, according to the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements. Either number makes sense only as a swap. A fish dinner helps when it takes the place of a meat dinner, not when someone adds it to an otherwise unchanged week. Each fish night also calls for only a modest portion, since an adult serving is 3 to 5 ounces. A reader who reliably eats fish instead of meat at the heart association's lower level is already making the trade, and Mayo Clinic's range is the level to aim toward.

Fish oil does not stand in for fish. The diet ties the benefit to fatty fish. Unless a person's cardiovascular risk is high, the American Heart Association doesn't advise taking omega-3 pills, according to the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements. A person who takes a capsule still eats the meat dinner, so the main part of the trade never happens. The cooking method matters too. "Stay away from deep-fried fish," Mayo Clinic staff said. Frying a fillet turns a fish night back into the kind of meal the pattern is meant to replace.

With seafood, the number is negotiable but the swap is not: grilled or baked fish on a night that would have been meat is the habit that counts.

Nuts belong in the Mediterranean diet as a backup source of fat, not the main one. Extra virgin olive oil does most of the work of supplying unsaturated fat at every meal, and nuts rank behind it as a supplier of that same fat. That supporting role keeps the portion small. The diet calls for nuts in four quarter-cup servings a week, according to Mayo Clinic. A quarter cup is a small handful, so each serving adds a modest amount to the fat a cook already gets from the oil in the pan. A reader who sprinkles almonds on a salad or eats a handful of walnuts in the afternoon is adding to the fat the oil already supplies, not building meals around nuts. Nuts matter here for the healthy fat they contribute to the whole pattern, more than for any food they replace.

The advice also covers a specific kind of nut, not every product that contains them. Only raw, unsalted nuts count toward the weekly target, so a can of salted cashews or a seasoned snack mix doesn't meet it as written. That gives the reader a clear way to divide the advice. The weekly count works as a target to aim near, much like the other serving numbers in the diet. The choice at the store is a firm requirement, and it is the one part of this advice that the reader controls completely every time they shop.

Nuts are the diet's measured extra: a small weekly portion that counts only when it is bought plain.

Mayo Clinic advises eating dairy only in moderate portions and choosing low-fat versions over higher-fat ones. The trade therefore happens inside the dairy case, with leaner products going in and richer ones coming out. For most people, the change would show up at breakfast, since whole milk and 2% milk are the products the advice asks them to cut back. The call for moderate portions and the call for low-fat products do not rest on equally firm proof, and a reader should know which one to hold tightly.

Recent research has called the low-fat rule into question. Participants in a recent trial ate full-fat dairy three times a day, according to the University of Toronto, and their results did not show the harm the low-fat rule is meant to prevent. "Those that had three servings of dairy didn't have adverse levels of blood cholesterol or lipids or evidence of insulin resistance," said Harvey Anderson, a professor of nutritional sciences at the Temerty Faculty of Medicine at the University of Toronto. Blood pressure also improved in the groups eating dairy, so on that measure the full-fat eaters did better rather than merely no worse. For these markers, choosing low-fat looks more like a precaution against a harm the trial did not find than a proven requirement.

That challenge still leaves moderation standing. The trial lasted only 12 weeks, according to the University of Toronto. That is long enough to show early changes in cholesterol and blood pressure but too short to show what years of full-fat dairy would do. Even the researcher questioning the low-fat rule does not argue for more dairy, since Anderson favors eating many different foods and limiting how much of any one goes on the plate. His view and Mayo Clinic's call for moderate portions point the same way, and the trial's three daily servings were an amount researchers tested, not a license.

Moderation is the rule worth keeping, though Mayo Clinic gives no daily or weekly count for it. Its one concrete gauge is the size of a single serving, which matches four dice, and readers have to judge for themselves how many of those servings still count as moderate.

Keep dairy portions modest even without a set number, and treat low-fat as a sensible default that researchers have reopened for debate, not as a rule to obey at all costs.

The Mediterranean diet asks people to eat less red and processed meat, but it stops short of banning it. Mayo Clinic advises reducing both, and the heart benefit of doing so depends on what takes the meat's place. Among people who ate pulses instead of all their red meat, the risk of ischemic heart disease was 10% lower, according to EMJ. The gain belongs to the trade, not to the cut alone. A plate with the meat simply removed does only half the job, and the empty space needs beans, lentils or other pulses.

The limit worth holding firmly is the one on processed meat. EMJ reports that ischemic heart disease risk ran 15% lower when pulses took the place of processed meat, and cardiovascular disease risk ran 13% lower when they stood in for processed poultry. Both results involve processed foods, whether red meat or poultry, so the common thread is processing rather than the color of the meat. These findings center on processed meat and say nothing on their own about whether fresh cuts carry the same risk. That fits the diet's own guidance, which still allows lean cuts in small portions.

The findings come from a large group of adults in the U.K., 118,272 people in the UK Biobank, according to EMJ. A link of this kind supports the trade, but it doesn't prove that eating pulses instead of processed meat is what lowers the risk.

The practical rule is to be strict about processed meat and to fill the gap with pulses. Lean fresh cuts in small portions are an allowance the diet makes on purpose, not a lapse to feel guilty about.

Eggs sit on the limited side of the Mediterranean diet, and the limit falls on the yolk rather than on the egg as a whole. Mayo Clinic allows up to four whole eggs a week, and it sets that limit because of the saturated fat in the yolk rather than because of any doubt about the egg's nutritional value. For most people the cap asks for little change, since typical intake already runs three to four eggs a week, according to TIME. A reader who follows it mainly avoids drifting toward an omelet every morning.

The heart risk behind the cap is real but small for any one person. In 2019, researchers estimated that an extra half egg a day would mean about 11 additional heart disease cases among every 1,000 people across 30 years, according to TIME. Nearly everyone in that group would see no difference, which makes this an effect that appears across a population rather than a sharp danger to each eater. The four-egg figure is a nutritionists' suggestion rather than a number taken from a study, and the science underneath it is open. "The existing literature is still controversial and inconclusive for nutrition experts and researchers to conclude the safety of eggs," said Victor Wenze Zhong, a postdoctoral fellow at Northwestern University's Feinberg School of Medicine and a co-author of the study behind that estimate. A fifth egg in one week breaks no rule. A steady climb past four, though, moves toward the intake the research flags.

The most useful trade happens inside the egg itself. Mayo Clinic's cap counts whole eggs and targets the fat in the yolk, while egg whites contain no cholesterol, according to TIME. Zhong himself recommends whites as a stand-in for whole eggs. A scramble made with one yolk and several extra whites keeps eggs on the plate without adding to the weekly yolk count.

Be strict about how many yolks you eat and relaxed about how many eggs.

Herbs and spices earn their place mainly for what they let a cook leave out of a dish. Mayo Clinic lists them among the foods the Mediterranean diet is most often high in, which places seasoning among the diet's regular foods rather than its occasional ones. The diet calls for them because a cook who seasons food richly needs less salt. The CDC names spices, garlic and citrus juice as substitutes for salt in cooking, so the swap is shared health advice rather than one diet's quirk. A cook who keeps those on hand has several familiar ways to make food taste full without the shaker.

The swap does less than it seems to, because the saltshaker was never where most of the salt came from. Packaged foods and restaurant meals supply most of the sodium people eat, the CDC reports, and bread adds more because people eat it often even though each serving carries only moderate sodium. Seasoning at home therefore trims only a minor share of a person's salt. Bread is also one of the foods the diet encourages, so a cook who seasons generously still takes in salt that no herb can remove. The full trade has two parts: season richly at home, and cut back on packaged foods, sauces and takeout.

Mayo Clinic sets no serving target for herbs and spices, so a reader has no amount to measure against. That fits the trade itself, because success shows in the salt left out rather than in the amount of seasoning put in.

Here a reader can be as generous as they like, and the strictness belongs to the salt hidden in the rest of their food.

Mormon Wives' Jen Affleck Alleges Zac Affleck Gambled Away Their Money

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County’s Jen Affleck claimed her husband Zac Affleck gambled all of their money, alleging he spent over $100,000 that was in their bank account.

Jen Affleck is an open book about what went wrong in her marriage to Zac Affleck.

Months before the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star filed for divorce from Zac after seven years of marriage, Jen—who is pregnant with the couple's fourth baby—detailed what led to their breakup on the spinoff series The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, claiming that her husband gambled away their savings.

And not only that, but Jen alleged he did it numerous times throughout their marriage.

“He was being really weird,” she shared during a conversation with her costar Bobbi Althoff in the fourth episode of the Hulu series, out Nov. 12. “It seemed like he just wasn't being honest. So, I went to check our bank. The money's gone, and there's over $100,000 in there. So, I called him and I'm like, ‘Why is the money gone?’”

“He’s done this before multiple times,” Jen continued, “whether it was through online poker or it was through investing in crypto, I said, ‘You're not allowed to do that anymore.“

Bobbi chimed in, asking an emotional Jen for clarification that Zac has “just spent all of your money” before, to which Jen replied, “Yes.”

Breaking down in tears, the 27-year-old went on to tell Bobbi that Zac “admitted” to emptying their bank account.

“He’s like, ‘I f--ked up and you wanted to separate, so I didn't tell you this because I knew if you found out you would divorce me,’” Jen shared. “And I said, ‘Yeah, I'm done. I'm divorcing you. F--k you. I’m literally pregnant.”

The MomToker added that she only took a closer look at the family’s finances when someone “came to Zac parents’ house yesterday and was trying to serve him papers.”

“They were looking for him,” she recalled. “He's like, ‘Yeah, well, remember we lived in that home in Arizona. I guess I didn't pay all the extra charges or whatever for us to move out.’ And I was like, ‘You told me you took care of that.’”

“Zac and I have moved a lot in the past couple of years,” Jen continued. “We rented a house in Arizona for a year. We ended up moving back to Utah. We literally moved out of that house in Arizona two years ago. So, I thought everything was taken care of. I mean, Zac was handling the finances during that time, but finding this out has made me question everything.”

And, according to the Dancing With the Stars alum, she’s still unsure about the extent of Zac’s alleged debts.

E! News has reached out to Zac for comment on the gambling allegations but has not heard back.

However, the 30-year-old recently addressed his split, sharing in a Nov. 5 TikTok, “Jen came to me about a year ago, after Dancing With the Stars, and told me she was unhappy and wanted a divorce. And I basically begged her to stay, told her work on things, fix things, become whoever she needed me to be."

"I don't want anyone speaking bad about Jen," he continued. "I'll be the first to say I've made plenty of mistakes in our marriage, and it just really was time for us to go our own ways. We got married so young, and it sucks because I really did feel like we had something pretty special when we got married."

Jen and Zac’s marital issues played out on their original Hulu show, with the pair ultimately deciding to move to Orange County for a fresh start. But by the spinoff’s first episode, Jen said she had already hit her “breaking point.”

“I never thought I’d be in the same situation again where I’d be on the verge of divorcing Zac and get pregnant again,” she admitted. “There’s already judgment around divorce when it comes to the church, throw on a pregnancy, and that’s gonna become worse.”

And as Jen enters her new chapter while preparing for the birth of baby no. 4, read on for where the rest of the SLOMW couples stand…

Taylor Frankie Paul & Dakota Mortensen: Broken Up

While Taylor Frankie Paul's romance with Dakota Mortensen—they welcomed son Ever in 2024—bloomed on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, they called it quits due to Dakota's infidelity.

"Right after Ever was born, Dakota and I were living together and in bliss with our new baby, and I was even considering engagement," Taylor—who began dating the real estate agent in July 2022—revealed during the season two premiere of the Hulu series. "And then I learned that he was lying this entire time."

On the series, Taylor said that Dakota accused Jenna of instigating their affair.

"No, it was him," she emphasized in the confessional. "He said he had just kissed her on the couch. No, he went on the couch, they hooked up on the couch, and then they went to the room and they hooked up."

Since ending her romance with the Basin Tiling founder, the mom of three (she shares two kids with ex-husband Tate Paul) was set to find love once again on season 22 of The Bachelorette, but things got a bit thorny when news broke in March 2026 of an alleged confrontation between the exes weeks earlier.

As for Dakota, he has kicked off a romance with show guest star Shinia Powell, though he previously apologized to Taylor for hooking up with her during a season four episode.

Whitney Leavitt & Connor Leavitt: Married

For instance, the Dancing With the Stars alum, who announced during her Broadway run in May 2026 that she'd be leaving Mormon Wives, had to shut down rumors about her husband's sexuality.

Through the ups and downs, the reality star emphasized that she and Connor—who were 23 and 22, respectively, when they got married—are stronger than ever. In fact, they even renewed their vows in February 2026 for their 10th wedding anniversary.

"We got married really young," Conner told Brides after their ceremony. "It felt like our wedding day, as much as it was for us, it really felt like it was for our families. So we really want to do something to make it ours, and this felt like a really fun way to do it."

Married in 2019, Jen Affleck and Zac Affleck—no relation to Ben Affleck, despite jokes to the contrary—have been at the center of breakup rumors since SLOMW's debut.

Mayci didn't expect to find love with Jacob Neeley. In fact, after the unexpected death of her boyfriend Arik Mack in 2015, when she was just 14 weeks pregnant with their son Hudson, she was prepared to be a single mom.

"Your ability to remain consistently loyal to your role as father/step-father while navigating all that life throws your way, will never cease to amaze me!"

Mikayla Matthews and Jace Terry have been through it all together. While The reality star first met Jace in 2015 when she was 15 and they welcomed their first son Beckham two years later. Since then, the couple—who tied the knot in 2018—welcomed kids Haven, Tommie and Lottie.

While Mikayla and Jace have been loving life with their four kids, Mikayla admitted that she hopes to grow their family even further.

"When people ask me how many kids I want, I'm like, 'I've always wanted three or four, but I could see myself having six,'" she told People in February 2025. "I'm excited to hopefully move into a new house. I want a big lot for my herd of kids."

As Mikayla began unpacking her trauma surrounding years of sexual abuse as a teen, as well as her chronic illness—which causes flare-ups due to heightened stress—their marriage had been deeply affected.

And, as she added, “He’s been very supportive.”

Though Miranda and Chase broke up in 2024, the former filed for divorce in 2026.
