‘Ebenezer’ Review: Johnny Depp Is Absolutely Insufferable in a Dull and Dreary Christmas Carol

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A Sauvage and jowly overactor in dire need of career rehabilitation could hardly ask for a better part to play than Ebenezer Scrooge. He’s a cruel miser of a man who comes to learn the error of his ways over the course of a ghost story that preys upon Christian precepts of redemption and forgiveness (along with the more secular warm and fuzzies of seeing a bad guy discover the joys of being good). Indeed, what is “cancel culture” if not a shackled delusion that visits self-made pariahs in the dead of night, and what is Charles Dickens’ “A Christmas Carol” if not a plea to believe that even the most odious of souls are still capable of being saved? Related Stories Sandra Wollner’s ‘Everytime’ Is a Stunning Film About Grief Like You’ve Never Seen Before How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Dominated the Box Office: GKIDS Chief on IMAX’s Impact and the Growing Market for Foreign-Language Movies

It’s been reported that Johnny Depp accepted the title role of Ti West’s “Ebenezer” at a fraction of his usual rate, but I suspect that he probably would have done this movie for free. While the embattled star earned more than $250 million for his work on the “Pirates of the Caribbean” franchise, even that psychotic number pales in comparison to the pricelessness of starring in a major studio Christmas movie that allows him to look just left of the camera and say lines like “I’m not the man I was!” and “I was once good, and I know I can be again!” before his character saves a cherubic little boy from certain death and invites a freezing beggar inside for a warm cup of tea. If an old crank like Scrooge can make his way back toward grace, why not the convicted star of “Mortdecai,” and the alleged perpetrator of several even more serious crimes?

Johnny Depp may prefer for that to be framed as a rhetorical question, but his insufferable turn as literature’s most cantankerous moneylender demands an honest answer. For me, that answer is rather straightforward: While Depp has never been the subtlest of actors, his post-Sparrow pivot away from artistic curiosity has distended his talents into such a grotesque caricature of their former charm that “Ebenezer” left me feeling like I may never be able to empathize with his screen presence again.

Scrooge is obviously a scenery-chewer’s dream, but the crescent arc he traces from grinch to Christmas miracle is only believable enough to sustain Dickens’ fable if you believe it on both ends. Depp’s performance — an advent calendar of empty affectations without a soul to anchor them, every shot full of face and bereft of feeling — makes that exceedingly difficult. His offscreen unsavoriness certainly doesn’t help, but there’s a more immediate problem when the lights go down, one that makes him a unique case among Hollywood’s tainted array of mega-star actors: He seems to have become very bad at acting. Whatever my feelings about Depp as a human being, the truth is that I didn’t buy him as a glowering monster any more than I bought him as a beaming man who’s grown “better than his word.”

An unengaging Ebenezer is a tough break for a movie called “Ebenezer,” and screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern’s fidelity to the source material doesn’t exactly leave West with a wealth of opportunities to compensate for such fatal miscasting. West’s take on the holiday classic is faithful to the core — no Muppets, no ghosts of girlfriends past, no uncanny mo-capped Jim Carrey. As you might expect from the director of indie genre standouts like “Pearl” and “The House of the Devil,” West’s big idea is to scare the Dickens out of us by turning up the volume on this story’s implicit scariness. Sometimes literally, as this PG-13 carol offers a small handful of gentle jump-scares.

“Ebenezer” isn’t a horror movie in the strictest sense, but West does what he can to play up the fear that eventually terrifies Scrooge into giving a shit about his fellow man. Most — or all — of those flourishes are purely cosmetic in nature, and hellbent on sucking every glimmer of life out of Victorian England. Scrooge’s office has never looked more haunted, as Checco Varese’s grimdark cinematography and Eve Stewart’s Yharnam-esque production design combine to create a London that’s in dire need of Christmas cheer (the vibe should go a long way with tweenage audiences eager for a bit of spice with their holiday viewing, though I suspect the whole thing is too staid and oppressive to become any kind of perennial favorite).

And since West would sooner die than pass up a chance for death, this version of the story treats us to a musty prologue where we actually get to see Ebenezer’s business partner bite the dust with his mouth wide open (Marley is played by Ian McKellen in what currently stands as the most “remember ‘The Christophers?’” performance of the year). From there, the action skips forward to Christmas Eve 1843, whence Ebenezer is inflicting his usual “Bah, humbug!” routine on poor Bob Cratchit (Rupert Grint), the underpaid clerk he resents for taking the next day off (HR being a decidedly 20th-century invention). But the fates have plans of their own for the bitter-cold businessman, who promptly pisses himself when the ghost of old Marley bursts into his miserable bedchamber later that night.

Reaction shots ensue, as Depp curdles his face into a series of the most exaggerated expressions since the death of silent cinema, every one of them betraying his character’s frigidness. This Ebenezer takes an active pleasure in being cruel, which not only makes him more detestable (which is fine), but also endows him with an innate capacity for joy that makes the rest of the movie feel like it’s walking in circles (which is less fine). Given that Depp’s performance is only as wide as his lips and as deep as his pockets, it seems wasteful that we should have to walk through the hollows of Ebenezer’s past, present, and future in order to fix him — it would be easier and equally meaningful to just push the corners of his mouth into a smile.

Of course, that would deprive us of the “holy shit, that’s Andrea Riseborough” fun of the “To Leslie” star’s Ghost of Christmas Past, a white-eyed ghoul who looks like a spirit from “The Conjuring” who popped up for a sudden jolt and then forgot to leave the frame. She’s the most amusing part of a familiar tour through the sadness of Ebenezer’s childhood, where he was bullied by the other kids and kept apart from his beloved older sister. Tramell Tillman is equally compelling as the boisterous Ghost of Christmas Present, though his bizarre character design — a round-bellied man whose face appears to be rotting to pieces in real-time — turns out to be reverse-engineered from an unnerving reveal that both honors Dickens’ text and also underlines West’s limited conviction to rejuvenate it with the visceral immediacy of a horror film (his commitment to the bit falls well short of the Robert Eggers-level follow-through that conceit demands).

Given the emphasis the director puts on the film’s genre spin, it’s ironic and surprising that “Ebenezer” is at its best when it focuses on the most basic human elements of its story, if only because the supporting characters offer such a welcome contrast to whatever Depp is doing. Sam Claflin and Daisy Ridley don’t have a whole lot to do as Ebenezer’s kind-hearted nephew Fred and his wife Clara (respectively), but the film takes pains to stress how sad it is that Scrooge is Fred’s last connection to his dead mother, and it’s touching to see how much it would mean for him to have a cordial relationship with the old miser.

It is considerably less touching to see Ebenezer arrive at a mutual feeling after palling around with the film’s Nazgûl-coded Ghost of Christmas Yet to Come (Robert Strange, cloaked in CGI), who forces the misanthrope to face the emptiness of his own miserable existence. “The flame of humanity still flickers within you,” someone tells Scrooge, but the slapstick pageantry of Depp’s performance sabotages the movie by keeping that flame at a low simmer from the start. I didn’t believe the fire had ever gone out any more than I believed that it was eventually rekindled, as this Ebenezer Scrooge is far too elastic and self-amused to convincingly suggest that he’s experiencing a permanent change of heart. He can’t even sustain a facial expression for more than a few seconds before he starts mugging around in search of another one.

There will, of course, be those who insist that I only panned “Ebenezer” because Johnny Depp is in it, and I suppose they’re at least half-right about that, if not quite for the reason they assume. The truth is that it would be a lot more interesting and true to the spirit of Dickens’ classic if I liked this movie in spite of Depp rather than disliking it because of him, as I can’t help but share the author’s opinion that everyone is theoretically capable of redemption, be it measure by measure or miraculously complete.

Alas, the actor’s “comeback” performance is just yet another thing that he ought to be sorry for.

Paramount Pictures will release “Ebenezer” in theaters on Friday, November 13.

Mortgage Rates Fall for Fourth Straight Week Despite Looming Fed Hike

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates continued their slow retreat for a fourth consecutive week, mirroring the trajectory of bond yields, although this week's hotter-than-expected inflation print suggests the relief will be short-lived.

The average rate on 30-year fixed home loans decreased to 7.11% for the week ending Nov. 12, down 7 basis points from 7.18% the week before, according to Freddie Mac. For perspective, rates averaged 6.24% one year ago.

"Mortgage rates dropped to their lowest level in seven weeks, a welcome development that gives borrowers greater flexibility as they navigate the current housing market," says Sam Khater, Freddie Mac's chief economist.

A month of falling mortgage rates comes as welcome news for homebuyers, though Realtor.com® economist Jiayi Xu warns that a reversal could come as soon as next week.

The stronger-than-forecast October jobs report showed the labor market holding up well, clearing the way for the Federal Reserve's next federal funds rate hike as part of its mandate to curb inflation.

Fed Governor Christopher Waller warned this week that a larger 50 basis point increase should be considered in December’s Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting if inflation remains hot.

Shortly after Waller's warning, the October Consumer Price Index report exceeded economists' expectations, pushing the 10-year Treasury yield toward 5%.

A December hike is now essentially priced in, with odds of a 25-basis-point increase topping 93%, according to CME FedWatch. The odds of another quarter-percentage-point hike in January 2027 currently stand at 67%.

"The debate is no longer if the Fed hikes, but how far and how fast," says Xu.

Today's homebuyers find themselves squeezed by both high mortgage rates and high inflation, and Xu contends that inflation is the bigger long-term problem.

"High rates raise monthly payments and shrink the loan a buyer can qualify for, and the recent pullback in pending sales shows how sensitive buyers are," notes the economist. "But persistent high inflation is what keeps the Fed hiking and rates elevated."

Crucially, inflation also erodes households' purchasing power, especially when wages lag consumer prices.

In October, existing-home sales dropped to their lowest level since Aug. 2010, according to the latest data  from the National Association of Realtors®.

In this economic environment, first-time buyers are hit the hardest because they have no existing equity to use as a cushion, forcing them to save toward a down payment from already-stretched budgets.

"Even if rates ease, living costs rising faster than pay will keep weighing on demand for years," says Xu.

Sellers have responded to cooling buyer demand by pulling back. This week, new listing activity fell 10.3% year over year after six weeks of more incremental declines, according to the latest weekly Realtor.com housing trends report.

"Sellers are discouraged because of how disengaged buyers are," says Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones. "The prices buyers are open to are less appealing to sellers than they were when mortgage rates were lower."

Your credit score plays a role when you apply for a mortgage. A credit score will determine whether you qualify for a mortgage and the interest rate you'll receive. The higher the credit score, the lower the interest rate you'll qualify for.

The credit score you need will vary depending on the type of loan. A score of 620 is a "fair" rating. However, people applying for a Federal Housing Administration loan might be able to get approved with a credit score of 500, which is considered a low score.

Homebuyers with credit scores of 740 or higher are typically considered to be in very good standing and can usually qualify for better rates, which can reduce monthly payments.

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.

NFL predictions: Our picks for Giants-Commanders, the full Week 10 slate

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The New York Giants and Washington Commanders kick off Week 10 of the NFL season Thursday night at what promises to be a rainy, chilly MetLife Stadium. Can the Giants solidify their NFC East lead, or will the race for the division title tighten? Here is how the Big Blue View staff sees that game, and the rest of the Week 10 matchups.

Which version of the Giants will show up on Thursday night?

Will we get the sloppy Giants that came out flat against the Philadelphia Eagles, or the tough, physical, and technically sound Giants that showed up against the Houston Texans? John Harbaugh's 9-1 record home record on Thursday Nights is encouraging.

I would say that this is a game the Giants should win on paper, particularly with Washington missing some of their best players and their starting center in the concussion protocol. However, I'm also not convinced that these Giants can play a normal game and just take care of business. I keep waiting for the Giants to play a complete game and execute on offense and defense, but this team seems unable to be both consistent and explosive.

I hope the offensive line comes prepared to bully an injured Washington defensive line and J.J. McCarthy isn't forced into low percentage throws every third down by long yardage situations. Likewise, I hope that Dennard Wilson comes out swinging on defense, but also has a counterpunch ready in case Jayden Daniels has an answer to the Cover-0 blitz.

I'm picking the Giants here, but they can't afford to take Washington lightly or give Jayden Daniels the chance to catch his breath and swing the game.

This is the Giants' second consecutive must-win game. You wouldn't think that when 5-2-1 plays 2-5-1, but (1) the Giants haven't beaten the Commanders since Jayden Daniels became their quarterback (even though Daniels didn't play in one of those losses), (2) despite their record, Washington has beaten the Seahawks and only narrowly lost to the 49ers and Rams while the Giants were beaten soundly by the Rams, the only good team they've faced, and still have the 49ers and Seahawks coming up, along with the Jaguars and Lions. A division loss greatly weakens their hold on the NFC East lead, their only real path to the playoffs.

Terry McLaurin is out for the season, but in his place is Stefon Diggs, who did not face the Giants in the first game. Jacory Crosky-Merritt, who beat the Giants' run D for a 33-yard TD right off the bat in the first game, is also out. The Commanders are further decimated by injuries since their last meeting to key starters such as DT Daron Payne and IOL Lucas Patrick. So really the Giants should prevail. The problem is…containing the running of Daniels, which they did not do well in their first meeting (103 yards, by far his highest of the season, and his only 2 rushing TDs of the season). Hopefully Dennard Wilson will have a plan for that. If so, and if the Giants execute it, then the Giants can win.

The rematch of the NFL's only tie game of the season will be played in prime time on Thursday night, and for the ninth time in nine games, the Giants are playing a team that doesn't have a winning record. But you can throw the records out the window: The 5-2-1 Giants, at home, are just 1.5 point favorites over the 2-5-1 Commanders. Go figure…

Big Blue won with defense and a strong kicking game on Sunday in Philadelphia, with Arvel Reese perhaps becoming a star in real time. It was an ugly win, with the offense producing just 19 points. The Giants will need more than that against Jayden Daniels and company on Thursday night. Cam Skattebo is expected back, and that's good news. New York couldn't run the ball on Sunday, which stagnated their offense. Right tackle Jermaine Eluemunor (calf) might miss this game, however, and the O-line struggled at Philadelphia as it was.

J.J. McCarthy wasn't terrible on Sunday, but he wasn't especially good either and now he's dealing with a hip pointer. Jameis Winston led New York on its only touchdown drive whileMcCarthy was temporarily knocked from the game. McCarthy hasn't thrown a TD pass (or an interception) in either of his two starts.

This figures to be a close game, just like the first one. It'll be fun to watch standout rookies Reese and Sonny Styles — college teammates at Ohio State last season — leading the way for each defense. Seriously – how did anyone score on the Buckeyes? Anyway, Dennard Wilson needs to find a way to keep Daniels from gashing his defense with his legs. He ran for 103 yards and two scores in the first game.

This game could come down to intangibles like turnovers and special teams. The G-Men have been winning (or tying) close games and that's a reflection on good coaching to a degree, and favorable luck. In a game that should be close, who am I to pick against that?

We are not picking games by the spread here, but I am surprised the Giants are just 1.5-point favorites. That indicates a continuing lack of real belief in the Giants in the betting market. The Giants understand the opportunity they have in front of them, and how important this game is.

In the rainy, windy, miserable conditions at MetLife Stadium Thursday evening I think you have to expect what has become a typical Giants game this season — close, somewhat sloppy, decided by who makes the plays and who properly handles the situations at the end of the game.

I have far more faith in the Giants to do that than I do the Commanders.

We all remember the Commanders stunning clock mismanagement at the end of the Week 5 tie with the Giants. That sort of questionable decision-making has continued to plague Washington, which is 1-3-1 in games decided by one score.

If you could the Arizona game, which I do since it was decided on the game's final play, seven of the Giants eight games this season were one-score games. The Giants are 5-1-1 in those games. They have lived on the edge, and have shown an ability to survive.

Is that formula sustainable forever? Maybe not. It does, though, make me feel good about the Giants' chances on Thursday.

Burnout is a workplace safety hazard. It’s time to start treating it like one

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Burnout is a huge workplace issue: the low engagement that comes with it costs every business affected a fortune. Worse yet, its effects on workers are deleterious to the point where it can literally lead to death.

Yet in my experience, most leaders consider “burnout” an HR line item to be managed by a wellness program. It’s hard to know how to jolt people out of this mindset, but if I were a CEO (and I am), I’d be very concerned about potential industrial manslaughter charges coming my way at worst, and shareholders rightfully wondering why I am failing to manage the main thing that’s materially impairing productivity at best.

Burnout is a workplace safety hazard—and needs to be governed as one. 

The World Health Organization defines burnout as the result of “chronic workplace stress that has not been successfully managed.” It’s a costly hazard that claims at least twice as many lives as workplace accidents. Long working hours alone led to an estimated 750,000 deaths from stroke and heart disease in 2016, more than twice the 360,000 people killed by workplace injuries, according to a report from WHO and the International Labour Organization. In the USA, workplace stressors are estimated to kill 120,000 people a year. 

Outside of the devastating human impact, the toll burnout takes on commercial performance should be triggering emergency board meetings all over the world. At an estimated cost of US $5 million a year for a company of 1,000 employees, it would be reasonable to ask very direct questions of the executive about their plans to arrest the poorly designed workplace practices that lead to burnout. 

Maybe the questions could start with who decided that it was okay to spend more than half of your working week in meetings or managing the almost 600 emails and more than 750 Teams messages knowledge workers field every week. Perhaps some questions about what the executive has done to deliberately design the working week toward outcomes—not admin. 

Burnout and chronic workplace stress come from the health of a company’s culture, and culture is almost universally dismissed, neglected or delegated to HR: A function with no formal training in organizational culture, no resources to shape it, and by its very station, no authority over the leadership behavior that does.

So, when an organization is faced with a growing number of employees taking chronic work stress-related leave—in Australia, the median time off for a serious mental health claim is almost five times that of other serious workplace injuries—it looks for a button to press, inside a function it believes has the answer. But doesn’t. 

HR has never had the answer and never will. The organizations that shape their culture with intention, and deliberately look at the accidental practices we’ve inherited from antique cultures, don’t start with HR. They look directly at:

  • the board, which needs to be asking the right questions, in line with increasing regulation and on behalf of shareholders looking for optimal productivity
  • the CEO, who is entirely accountable for the productivity and performance of the organization
  • the leaders who shape culture through the expectations they set, the practices they perpetuate, the consequences they apply for culture drift, and the chronic workplace issues they walk past every single day

CEOs and organizations never set out to burn their people out. It’s more a matter of ‘out of sight, out of mind’ and neglect. Neglecting the implications of workplace practices, neglecting better ways of working that are confirmed by research rather than the assumptions of a post-industrial era, and neglecting to look through the human lens at a growing contributor to cardiovascular incidents and death by suicide.

Whether leaders look at burnout through a productivity lens or from a human suffering perspective, the situation is clear. Leaders are enabling the ongoing, systemic impairment of commercial performance at the cost of human lives…and feeling OK about it because they’ve passed a memo to HR to invest in a wellness program or resilience training.

Thankfully, regulators and standard setters understand the crisis better and have begun something of a reckoning. ISO 45003 became the first international standard for managing psychological health and safety at work in 2021, and the US Surgeon General’s Framework for Workplace Mental Health and Well-Being names protection from harm, psychological safety included, as its first essential.

Australia has moved from guidance to enforcement. Every Australian state and territory now requires employers to identify and manage psychosocial hazards in the same way as physical ones, and in December 2025, Australia’s Department of Defence became the first federal employer convicted for failing to manage psychosocial risk.

Burnout is, quite literally, an existential risk. It’s time organizations truly started acting like it.

If this article raises concerns for you or someone you know, support is available. In the USA, call or text the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline on 988. In Australia, call Lifeline on 13 11 14.

The Best Rain Jackets for Hikers Who Always Overheat

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Picture this: You’re halfway through a 20-mile day when the sky opens up, dumping sheets of rain on you and your hiking partners. You pull out your shell, zip it up, and keep crushing. An hour later, you notice you’re starting to feel awfully damp. Your shell isn’t leaking, but it might as well be: Your exertion has turned it into a sweatbox.

The best rain jackets are next to useless if you end up sweating them out. If you’re the kind of hiker who always steams up your shell, picking one that has the breathability and venting options to keep you cool and dry is a must. We went through our testers’ favorite rain shells of 2026 and chose the best picks for hikers who always overheat.

Weight: 15.3 oz (men’s) / 13.9 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s S-XXXL / Women’s XXS-4X

Pros and Cons + Very breathable + Slightly stretchy + Certified carbon neutral – Too slim for layering

Our favorite rain jacket on the market today is also the most breathable we’ve tested: After wearing the Aspire (the Foray is the men’s equivalent) on a steep, 7-mile hike into a cabin in Alaska’s Chugach National Forest, tester Kate Paskievitch reported that she stayed dry the entire time—even with 40 pounds on her back. Outdoor Research’s AscentShell Dry, a 40-denier, 100-percent recycled three-layer nylon fabric, helped, but credit mostly goes to the full-length side zippers, which helped us bleed off heat quickly.

Weight: 10.9 oz (men’s) / 9.9 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s XS-XXL / Women’s XS-XXL

Pros and Cons + Excellent adjustability + Superior breathability + Dialed-in details – Expensive

Let’s acknowledge the elephant in the room first: Any rain jacket that costs $600 had better be really good. Fortunately, this model from Canada-based 7mesh delivers. Diagonal pit zips kept us from sweating out the Guardian Apex, even when our testers’ arms were down. And the nylon face fabric was both surprisingly breathable and tough enough that it remained snag-free, even after a long bushwhack through brambles.

Weight: 5.8 oz (men’s) / 5.5 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s S-XXL / Women’s XS-XL

Pros and Cons + Very light and packable + Partially plant-based – No hand pockets

At just 5.8 ounces for a men’s medium—roughly the same as the ever-popular Frogg Toggs Ultra Lite—the Marmot SuperAlloy Bio takes up barely any space, making it an ideal just-in-case piece to tuck into your pack. And it performs, too: The 2.5-layer NanoPro fabric kept our tester dry even after she wore it under a heavy pack in 50-degree temperatures, but was still tough enough that it didn’t fray after heavy use.

Weight: 25.4 oz (men’s) / 25.0 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s S-XXL / Women’s XS-3XL

Pros and Cons + Soft and supple + Lots of venting options + Easy to repair and actually recyclable – Heavy and bulky – Too warm for summer weather

The Velez Adventure Smock is not the lightest jacket in our test: The jacket, which eschews a waterproof-breathable membrane for a DWR-treated polyester face backed by wicking fabric, is better suited for colder-season temperatures. So what makes it a good jacket for hikers who run hot? In shoulder-season conditions, it breathes and maintains its waterproofing better than most traditional three-layer rain shells. Venting helps, too: Biceps zippers and a horizontal front flap across the torso make it easy to dump heat quickly before users get too steamy.

Weight: 1.8 oz (men’s) Sizes: Men’s XS-XXL

Pros and Cons + Ultralight + Surprisingly durable + Surprisingly weatherproof – No hood or chest pocket – Tight fit

At 1.8 ounces, this gossamer hoodless jacket is a reliable friend for mountain runners, fastpackers, and anyone who values velocity. The 10-denier recycled nylon exterior packs up to (literally) pocket size, and while we wouldn’t bet on it to keep you bone-dry through a multi-day storm, it handled surprise desert downpours without batting an eye. Breathability is exceptional, which our tester confirmed after a full day of biking along the Grand Canyon’s South Rim.

First, understand you may have to compromise. While waterproof-breathable membranes are almost ubiquitous, most jackets prioritize waterproofing over breathability, or vice versa. Jackets that can weather a downpour without so much as wetting out may not breathe well, and those that breathe well may be better for scattered showers than the kind of rain that makes you start thinking about building an ark.

Pay attention not just to the breathability of the jacket itself, but to the venting options, too. Even the most breathable fabrics can’t dump heat as well as a set of pit zips or mesh-backed pockets that expose your underlayers to fresh air. Packability is more important than you might think, too: If you’re consistently hot, having a jacket that you can ball up and stick inside your pack without a second thought is a better move than carrying a heavy, bulky one.

Our lead testers for men’s and women’s rain jackets are Drew Zieff and Elizabeth Kwak. Our gear editors pulled these recommendations from their original testing notes and scores to recommend the best rain jackets for hikers who overheat.

Hyatt Hotel Promises Jonathan Adler Toiletries—But There’s Drugstore Shampoo On Its Housekeeping Cart

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hotel Avante in Mountain View, California advertises Jonathan Adler bath amenities. A reader photographed its housekeeping cart with a retail bottle of HASK shampoo resting among unbranded squeeze bottles. This is another JdV by Hyatt property, just a few miles from the Wild Palms hotel where I reported on DRIFT bulk toiletries on housekeeping carts while the guest bathroom bottles displayed Jonathan Adler branding. Apparently readers need to keep checking what’s on the carts. The brown bottle is HASK Tea Tree Oil & Rosemary Invigorating Shampoo, in the 12-ounce size. HASK is an affordable retail hair care brand, with products sold at CVS and other stores. Around it are condiment-style squeeze bottles. At least one has “Body wash” handwritten on tape. That identifies the type of product, without telling guests whose product it is. It seems like Hyatt a good reason to check what this hotel is using to refill its toiletries. But they had this reason at its sister property, too. There needs to be much stronger monitoring. Hyatt’s Hotel Avante page lists “Jonathan Adler bath amenities” across all six room categories. The hotel’s separate website promises that as well. I have no particular opinion on HASK shampoo. My issue is that a hotel should deliver the product they specifically advertise to guests. And if the bulk toiletries in the room say they’re a specific brand, they should be that brand. Sures describes Avante as Wild Palms’ sister property and they’re both part of the same ownership group. In November 2025, I published a reader’s photos from Wild Palms. The bathroom had Jonathan Adler bottles, while the housekeeping cart carried large jugs of DRIFT Hydrated Body Care. Then in January, Ben at One Mile at a Time published follow-up photos showing the bulk jugs with their labels removed. Wild Palms still advertises Jonathan Adler toiletries. I just wrote about the ketchup, mustard and mayonnaise bottle approach to hotel toiletries. Once the hotel keeps reusing the branded packaging, it can change the contents without changing anything the guest sees. An owner who substitutes a cheaper product keeps the savings, while Hyatt and the advertised toiletry company risk their reputations. Remind me again why bulk dispensers were a better idea than single use biodegradable packaging? Topics on this pageHotel AvanteHyattJonathan AdlerCaliforniaCVS Pharmacy+2 more More From View from the Wing

This is another JdV by Hyatt property, just a few miles from the Wild Palms hotel where I reported on DRIFT bulk toiletries on housekeeping carts while the guest bathroom bottles displayed Jonathan Adler branding. Apparently readers need to keep checking what’s on the carts.

The brown bottle is HASK Tea Tree Oil & Rosemary Invigorating Shampoo, in the 12-ounce size. HASK is an affordable retail hair care brand, with products sold at CVS and other stores. Around it are condiment-style squeeze bottles. At least one has “Body wash” handwritten on tape. That identifies the type of product, without telling guests whose product it is.

It seems like Hyatt a good reason to check what this hotel is using to refill its toiletries. But they had this reason at its sister property, too. There needs to be much stronger monitoring.

Hyatt’s Hotel Avante page lists “Jonathan Adler bath amenities” across all six room categories. The hotel’s separate website promises that as well.

I have no particular opinion on HASK shampoo. My issue is that a hotel should deliver the product they specifically advertise to guests. And if the bulk toiletries in the room say they’re a specific brand, they should be that brand.

Sures describes Avante as Wild Palms’ sister property and they’re both part of the same ownership group. In November 2025, I published a reader’s photos from Wild Palms. The bathroom had Jonathan Adler bottles, while the housekeeping cart carried large jugs of DRIFT Hydrated Body Care.

Then in January, Ben at One Mile at a Time published follow-up photos showing the bulk jugs with their labels removed. Wild Palms still advertises Jonathan Adler toiletries.

I just wrote about the ketchup, mustard and mayonnaise bottle approach to hotel toiletries. Once the hotel keeps reusing the branded packaging, it can change the contents without changing anything the guest sees. An owner who substitutes a cheaper product keeps the savings, while Hyatt and the advertised toiletry company risk their reputations. Remind me again why bulk dispensers were a better idea than single use biodegradable packaging?

Measles outbreaks in 2026: the key numbers from the CDC's case tracker

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The CDC counted 5,182 U.S. measles cases by Nov. 5, 2026. Nine key numbers show who is sick, who is hospitalized and what is at stake.

Most Americans think of measles as a disease from an earlier era, one that vaccines dealt with long before their own children started school. For most of this century, the record supported that view. Between 2000 and 2024, the worst year was 2019, with 1,282 cases by one count from the CDC, although the agency's figures for that year differ slightly from page to page. Even that peak was small for a nation this size, and it showed measles as a rare, contained event until very recently. Against that baseline, the past two years stand out. Case counts climbed quickly, and they have not fallen back to the old level. The 2026 numbers keep raising one question: whether the country is living through a bad stretch or watching measles settle back in to stay.

More is at stake than a grim tally, because the country's standing as a place where measles no longer circulates is under formal review. A decision from the Pan American Health Organization on that elimination status is expected next week, according to Reuters. NBC 26 reports that Canada lost its own status last year. For the U.S., losing the status is a near and concrete possibility with a precedent next door. No ruling has been issued, so nothing is lost yet. The open question is whether the numbers already show the country failing the test. Whatever the ruling, those numbers show how far protection has slipped in parts of the country. That slippage matters more to families than any official label, because it decides where measles can spread next.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention built its measles tracker to show how large U.S. measles activity was in 2026 and what that activity was made of. Its figures show who is catching the disease, how many patients end up in the hospital, and how far vaccination among kindergartners has slipped. Read together, the figures do more than tally a bad year. They trace measles through the people left without protection, and the slides that follow pick up that thread.

The U.S. has recorded more measles cases in 2026 than in all of 2025, with about two months of the year still to count. Health officials confirmed 5,182 cases by Nov. 5, more than double the total for all of last year, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Officials reached that mark before the year was out, while the 2025 figure covers a full 12 months. They can only add cases from here, which makes "more than double" the minimum for the year rather than its final result.

A large part of that record comes from just two states. Pennsylvania has counted 1,487 cases, according to state health department figures reported by Reuters, and South Carolina has recorded 670. Pennsylvania's count comes from the state rather than the CDC, but it places a large part of the national record inside a single state. Together, those two states carry a major share of this year's cases, and the rise has hit some parts of the country far harder than others.

Almost everyone who caught measles in 2026 lives in the U.S. International visitors account for only 18 of the more than 5,000 confirmed cases. Measles is easy to picture as something travelers bring in from abroad, but this year's cases come from outbreaks inside American communities.

For anyone weighing their own risk, gaps in local vaccination matter far more than who crosses the border.

Nearly every measles case in the U.S. this year has been tied to an outbreak. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 96% of 2026 cases were linked to outbreaks, and 43 new outbreaks began during the year. The isolated case, in which one person picks up the virus and no one else gets sick, has almost vanished from the count. In a year driven by outbreaks, the danger is concentrated in the communities where a cluster has formed, while cases elsewhere remain rare.

Fewer new outbreaks began in 2026 than in 2025, when there were 48, yet this year's case count still climbed past last year's. Part of the explanation is that some outbreaks never ended. This year's count includes 1,385 cases from outbreaks that began in 2025, so a large share of the 2026 record was already underway before the year started. The record reflects outbreaks that kept going from last year as well as the ones that started this year. When the same chain of infection continues from one calendar year into the next, measles has become a lasting problem in those communities rather than a passing one.

Health officials in each state now hold the most current view of these outbreaks and run the response to them. The national count is a running total for the year, and the CDC itself says state health departments keep more current figures on the outbreaks within their borders. Pennsylvania is home to the country's biggest outbreak, according to Contagion Live. In New York, Gov. Kathy Hochul responded in October by declaring a disaster emergency for the state. Anyone living close to one of these clusters should check with their own state health department for the newest numbers.

Most people picture measles as a childhood illness that passes through classrooms and daycare rooms. In 2026, though, adults accounted for as much of it as school-age children did. Adults 20 and older and children ages 5 to 19 each made up 41% of confirmed cases through Nov. 5, according to preliminary figures from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, and children under 5 were the smallest group at 18%. Measles this year is not mainly a worry for parents of young children, and grown-ups make up as large a part of the spread as students do.

The large adult share came late in the year. Adults made up 29% of cases across all of 2025. Their share was 30% in July 2026, according to HealthDay. For the adult share of the full year to reach 41% by November, the cases recorded from summer onward must have involved adults more often than the earlier ones did. Any single age breakdown is a snapshot, and the groups that see new cases can keep changing as long as transmission continues. That shifting profile is a sign of a disease that has settled in, not an outbreak that comes and goes.

The wide range of ages among patients is evidence of a gap in protection that opened over many years, not in a single season. "That tells us this has been years of undervaccination in some communities," Dr. William Moss, a professor of epidemiology at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, told HealthDay. The people left unprotected over those years now include older children and adults, not just the newest group of kids.

In communities where vaccination rates have fallen, no age group is safe, and the question that matters is how well each community kept up its vaccinations.

This year's record measles count does not mean the vaccine has stopped working. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reports that 96% of 2026 cases were in people who were unvaccinated or whose vaccination status was unknown. Only 4% of patients were known to have been vaccinated, according to NBC 26. The disease is spreading among people who never got the shot or whose protection nobody can confirm, and it is rarely breaking through the vaccine.

People who finished the full two-dose course barely appear in this year's count, making up just 2% of 2026 cases. For a family deciding on the shot, completing both doses puts a child in the group that has stayed almost entirely out of this year's outbreaks.

The same lopsided split held through last year's outbreaks. In 2025, 93% of cases were unvaccinated or of unknown status. This year's share is higher still, so cases have stayed concentrated among the unprotected for two straight years, and measles has not moved into the vaccinated population in any meaningful way. Two years of the same pattern point to gaps in vaccination, not a weakness in the vaccine, as the reason measles keeps spreading.

The 2026 case count amounts to a tally of the people that vaccination has not reached.

Hospitals have treated far more measles patients this year, even though a person who catches the disease is no more likely to need a hospital than a year ago. About 11% of this year's cases needed hospital care, the same share as in 2025, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The jump in hospital patients comes from the jump in cases, with 562 measles patients hospitalized so far this year, up from 244 in all of 2025. A steady rate can sound like reassurance. With an outbreak that keeps growing, the same risk still means more patients in hospital beds every month the spread continues.

For someone with no vaccine protection, the chance of a hospital stay is much higher than 11%. Dr. Dennis Wang, an Epidemic Intelligence Service officer at the CDC, and his colleagues report that approximately 20% of unvaccinated U.S. measles patients were hospitalized. The 11% figure counts everyone who caught the virus, including people with some protection and mild cases that may barely register. That is why the national figure can be lower than the risk facing one unprotected child. The unchanged national rate is not a sign that measles is getting milder. It reflects this year's mix of patients, not a fixed trait of the disease.

The patients who did end up in the hospital were often seriously ill. In the west Texas outbreak, most of the hospitalized patients whose records were reviewed needed extra oxygen, according to the CDC. None of those patients was admitted only to keep the virus from spreading to others. Each of those hospital stays reflected the patient's own illness.

The west Texas records cover only part of one outbreak, but there, at least, a hospital bed went to a patient who was seriously ill.

School-age children are the least likely to end up in the hospital with measles, and the odds rise sharply at both ends of the age range. This year 17% of infected children under 5 and 15% of infected adults were hospitalized, compared with 4% of school-age children, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. For parents, a preschooler with measles faces far higher odds of a hospital stay than a 10-year-old does, and grown-ups face odds nearly as high as toddlers. In west Texas, the most serious hospital stays involved children. The CDC reports that all four hospitalized patients admitted to intensive care in the early months of that outbreak were under 18. The number is small, but no hospitalized adult in that period needed that level of care.

The adult rate deserves close attention, because adults make up a growing share of patients the longer an outbreak lasts. By the end of the west Texas outbreak, adults accounted for 32.4% of all cases, according to the CDC. Because hospital stays are common among infected adults, a long outbreak moves many more people into an age group that often needs hospital care. If this year's outbreaks keep running, more adults may end up in the hospital, although that remains a possibility rather than a measured trend.

Some adult hospital stays may reflect caution rather than severe illness. The CDC reports that four of the five adults hospitalized in the early months of the west Texas outbreak were women in the third trimester of pregnancy, and that none of those women developed pneumonia or low oxygen levels. Part of the adult hospital rate may therefore come from precautionary care during pregnancy rather than from measles at its worst, though that evidence rests on a handful of patients in a single outbreak.

The very young are hospitalized most often and adults nearly as often, but in west Texas the patients who needed intensive care were all children.

More Americans have died of measles this year than in all of 2025, and the year still has weeks to run. The CDC counts four measles deaths in 2026 as of Nov. 5, compared with three for the whole of last year. Even before December arrives, 2026 is already the deadlier year. "This number is subject to change; CDC will update its reporting as additional information becomes available and relevant reviews are completed," the CDC said. Readers should treat four as the least that has happened so far rather than as a settled total.

The number of measles deaths this year depends on who does the counting. Pennsylvania announced a measles death on Sept. 30 that brought the nation's toll to five, HealthDay reports, while the national count at the time listed two because the CDC uses its own rule to decide which deaths to include. State announcements and the federal tally can therefore give different totals for the same year. Readers should expect the federal figure to sit below what states report.

The deaths have come from the places where outbreaks have grown largest. Pennsylvania, where the most recent death was announced, has the largest active outbreak in the U.S., according to HealthDay. While outbreaks of that size remain active, the toll remains open.

The number to watch is not whether the count rises, but where it rises next.

Fewer kindergartners are vaccinated against measles than the level needed to keep the disease from spreading. The CDC reports that 92.4% of children starting school in the 2025–2026 school year had their MMR shots, down from 95.2% in 2019–2020. Protecting a community from measles takes coverage above 95%, according to the CDC. In about six years, the country went from just above that line to below it. A shortfall of a few points matters with measles because the protective line is set so high, and even a small gap leaves enough unprotected children for one case to lead to others. The gap is large in human terms: approximately 280,000 children in kindergarten classes during 2025–2026 were at risk.

The national rate still understates the danger, because people catch measles from those around them, and vaccination levels differ from one community to the next. "At local levels, vaccine coverage rates may vary considerably, and pockets of unvaccinated people can exist in states with high vaccination coverage," the CDC said. The CDC lists lower kindergarten coverage and rising measles activity around the world as the reasons for the increase in cases. Lower coverage at home does not cause the cases that arrive from abroad. It determines whether one of those cases stays isolated or leads to an outbreak, depending on how many people near the patient are vaccinated. Large outbreaks can therefore happen despite only a modest national decline, wherever unvaccinated children are grouped in particular schools and towns.

For parents, the vaccination rate at their own child's school tells them more than the national figure does.

Measles did not drop out of American life on its own. Public health officials declared it eliminated because Americans vaccinated their children year after year until the virus could no longer circulate. The CDC reports that the vaccine was licensed in 1963 and that elimination was declared in 2000, after decades of vaccination. The CDC lists three conditions behind that result: an effective vaccine, high vaccination coverage among children and a strong system for responding to cases. The country earned the status by keeping enough children protected, not because the disease vanished from the world. Because elimination depends on that coverage, the country can lose it the same way it won it.

"Eliminated" sounds final, but the standard behind the word is narrow. A country keeps the label as long as measles has not spread continuously within its borders for a year or longer. A country can record imported cases and even outbreaks and still qualify, as long as each chain of spread ends within a year. A high case count alone does not decide the question. The answer depends on whether any chain of infection in the U.S. now runs unbroken for a year. That puts the weight on the outbreaks that carried over from 2025 rather than on the size of this year's total.

The weak point is how many people get the vaccine, not how well it works. Two MMR doses are about 97% effective, the CDC reports, and nearly everyone who receives both doses stays protected. Too few children are getting their shots across much of the country, and in 22 states fewer than 90% of children born in 2021 have received the MMR vaccine. Coverage was one of the three conditions behind elimination, and as it weakens, the year-long chains of infection that the standard rules out have more room to form.

Travelers $TRV will keep bringing measles in from abroad whatever the ruling, so the country's standing was always going to depend on how many people at home stay vaccinated.

Phoebe Bridgers Announces The Lost Tour 2027 Dates

Just weeks after Phoebe Bridgers completed The Lost Tour 2026, the singer has announced a slate of 2027 dates in support of her latest album Lost Weekend.

Tesla and Cars &amp; Coffee Organizers Sued After Alleged 83-MPH Test Drive Ends in Head-On Crash

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A California woman claims a Tesla representative was showing off a Model Y's performance for photographers when he crossed into oncoming traffic and struck her SUV.

A 70-year-old California woman who reportedly suffered serious injuries in a head-on collision with a Tesla Model Y in the Bay Area this summer has filed a lawsuit against the automaker, the driver, and the organizers of a nearby Cars & Coffee event.

According to a civil complaint filed in San Mateo County Superior Court, a Tesla representative was demonstrating the vehicle's performance for event photographers. The driver allegedly reached speeds exceeding 80 miles per hour on a stretch of road with posted speed limits of 35–40 mph; the complaint claims he lost control on a blind curve, and crashed into Michelle Brooks's Honda CR-V.

The incident occurred on August 30, 2026, according to the filing, along State Route 84 near La Honda, California. The lawsuit identifies the Tesla driver as 21-year-old Micah Zuglian, who was allegedly driving a company-owned Model Y while attending a Cars & Coffee gathering at Ambassador Wine Garden. According to the complaint, Zuglian took two photographers associated with the event for a drive to demonstrate the Tesla's performance. Images allegedly captured during the drive show the vehicle's speedometer displaying 83 mph; another image reportedly shows a speed of 61 mph near a posted 35-mph speed limit sign.

The lawsuit alleges that Zuglian subsequently crossed the double-yellow lines on the road and struck Brooks's 2014 Honda CR-V head-on. Brooks had to be transported to Stanford Hospital for treatment of her injuries, which reportedly included fractured vertebrae, fractured ribs, broken limbs, and a traumatic brain injury.

The complaint alleges that representatives from the California Highway Patrol, San Mateo County Sheriff's Office, and a county supervisor's office met with event organizers beforehand and urged them to cancel the gathering over traffic safety concerns. The suit claims that roads in the area are notorious for racing, and that in the past five years, more than 20 people have died and almost 800 have been injured in automobile crashes in the vicinity.

Brooks alleges that Tesla negligently entrusted the Model Y to its representative and failed to adequately supervise him. The complaint also alleges negligence by the event organizers for proceeding despite the reported safety concerns. The plaintiff is seeking damages and requesting a jury trial.

The lawsuit also names Ethan Garabedian, doing business as “9burgring,” which organized the gathering, and Ambassador Wine Bars LLC, which hosted it. The allegations have not been proven in court, and the filing of a lawsuit does not establish liability on the part of Tesla, Zuglian, or the event organizers.

Probably Stolen Lou quest walkthrough: How to complete and all rewards

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The game’s most wanted criminal, no NPC in Probably Stolen can rival or challenge the mysterious (& dangerous) Lou.

And as you can expect, her cryptic persona persists in her quest, which can only be triggered and completed by playing a dangerous game. Here’s a full walkthrough on how to complete the Lou storyline in Probably Stolen.

Unlike most NPCs, like Serpico and Eleanor, Lou only appears on a random day after you purchase the Business Permit, available in the fifth week. During her visit, she will introduce herself before asking you for a series of ”tools” to be used on her next job. To trigger her quest and start her storyline, call Security on her and claim the 1.500 Credits bounty.

Now that you surprised Lou and made the questionable choice of turning in a specialist, a Republic Operative will pay you a visit in the next week, claiming Lou to be a traitor to the Republic and an alleged terrorist. He also gives you a Republic Transmitter and makes an offer: plant it into something and give it to Lou for 700 Credits, as she is sure to visit you again.

Sure enough, Lou will surprise you in a few weeks, inviting you to play a game and letting you know what’s at stake. The next morning, she reveals that a bomb is hidden within your wares and gives you until midnight to disarm it. Upon review, the Plastic Explosive will prove to be fake.

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

Following your fake brush with death, Lou will pay a visit to the store in around five days and leave her ”Box of Toys” in your care, before warning you not to touch it. The box features a document revealing more about her past and ties with the Republic.

She returns to grab it the next day. If you haven’t moved the contents of the box (you can still read the document at no risk) or placed the transmitter in it, she allows you to pick either 1x Advanced Turbo Booster, 1x Smuggler’s Bay, 1x Backpack (Small), or 2x Module Expansion Kits as a reward.

Following the encounter above, just keep playing until Lou visits under the guise of an inspector. She reveals that her ”guest’ has finally given her the info she needs, before asking for 750 Credits (or 500 depending on your chosen difficulty) worth of weapons, armor, or meds as a trade for the bounty you received earlier. You have one week to secure it. If you can’t, she asks for the amount in Credits on the due date.

We are now at the final stretch of her storyline, as Lou will thank you and vanish upon getting the agreed-upon ”tools” (or the necessary Credits) in her next visit. This will be your final meeting, with the news of the assassination of an admiral stamping the papers the next morning. She will send you a note, though, together with a Storage Bay Expansion Kit.

Lou’s quest/storyline then ends with another visit from the Republic Operative, who reveals they plan to pursue her and hints at your involvement in the whole ordeal.

With Lou’s quest done, don’t forget to also check out how to produce Huge Nutrifruits and unlock the Green Thumb achievement.

He joined the Destructoid team in May 2024 as a Contributing Writer and can always be found either thinking about Final Fantasy VII or eagerly waiting for news on Kingdom Hearts IV.

8 findings from the EPA on radon, the invisible gas in your home

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Eight findings from the EPA's radon page cover who bears the risk, how the toll estimates changed, and why a home test matters.

Radon can't be seen, so a family can live for years with the gas in an ordinary house and never know it is there. Researchers agree that radon gives people lung cancer, according to the US EPA. The uncertainty lies in the size of the toll. The yearly death figure that health agencies cite is a best estimate drawn from a model, not a count of people who died. The open questions are how many people radon kills and which people. A household can't wait for those answers before finding out whether it is exposed.

Protection against radon has to come before the disease, because lung cancer is hard to catch in time. From 2019 to 2023, close to half of lung cancer cases had already reached distant organs by the time doctors diagnosed them, according to the CDC. Many cases of the disease can be prevented, and keeping radon out of the home does more good than counting on doctors to find the cancer early. A household also can't rely on a neighbor's result, because radon levels in homes next door can differ very much, according to the NIH. Late diagnosis and a neighbor's reading both leave a family without an answer. A test of its own home is the practical line of defense.

The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency set out to show how radon in homes causes lung cancer and how serious that harm is. Each of those findings matters less on its own than in combination. A reader who weighs them together learns who carries radon's risk and how firm the numbers behind it are, and that is enough to decide whether to test.

Smoking is not the only major cause of lung cancer in the U.S., and the next one on the list is a gas inside ordinary homes. Only smoking causes more lung cancer than radon, and for Americans who have never smoked, radon causes more than anything else, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. About 21,000 Americans die each year of lung cancer caused by radon. Radon is a cause in its own right rather than a side note to smoking, and a family can live with it without knowing.

The CDC cites the same estimate of 21,000 deaths a year. That figure is a midpoint, and the EPA's full estimate runs from 8,000 to 45,000 deaths a year. The true toll could be well below the familiar number or well above it, and nobody can yet say which. Even the low end of that range means thousands of deaths a year. No household has a reason to put off learning its own level while the experts work out the national number.

Cigarette smoking has become uncommon among U.S. adults, only 12.5% of whom smoked in 2020, according to the CDC. Yet about 2,900 radon deaths a year occur among people who never smoked, and those deaths don't depend on anyone's cigarette use. Doctors who treat the disease see it changing. "This is not your grandfather's lung cancer," said Dr. Timothy Mullett, medical director of the Markey Cancer Center Affiliate Network at the University of Kentucky.

Those deaths among never-smokers can come down only through repairs to the house.

Lung cancer leaves few of its patients alive for long, and that is why radon's harm is counted in deaths rather than in illnesses people recover from. The EPA puts the share of patients who live five years past their diagnosis at 11 to 15 percent. Most lung cancer patients never reach a point where they have been treated and are well again.

That low survival, repeated across a large number of patients, produces a death toll that returns every year. In 2024, 128,663 Americans died of lung cancer, according to the CDC. With so few patients surviving five years, those deaths are the expected result of the disease rather than a handful of unlucky cases. Radon-related cases are part of that national toll, not a separate one, and with survival this low, each new case is more likely to end in death than in recovery.

Measured in deaths, radon is a bigger lung cancer threat than the secondhand smoke many people already go out of their way to avoid. The agency estimates that about 21,000 people die each year of lung cancer from radon, while the yearly toll it ties to secondhand smoke comes to an estimated 3,000. Radon gas in a basement deserves at least the caution people show toward a smoker in the room.

In a disease this hard to survive, a household gains the most by lowering its exposure before any cancer starts, and a radon test shows whether there is exposure to lower.

For a smoker, radon is not a separate hazard added to the cigarettes. The risks from the two are compounded, and the danger from each is greater when the other is present. The EPA estimates that about 62 in 1,000 smokers could develop lung cancer in homes with radon at 4 pCi/L. NIH reports that the EPA wants homes repaired once radon reaches 4 pCi/L or above, and that smokers account for most of the people who die of radon-related cancer. For anyone who smokes, the official line for action already marks serious risk rather than giving an early warning. In any household, the smoker has the most riding on the radon reading.

A reading under the action level is not a safe reading for a smoker. For readings from 2 to 4 pCi/L, the EPA's advice is that homeowners think about fixing the house, according to NIH. Yet at 2 pCi/L, the lowest reading in that band, about 32 smokers in 1,000 could develop lung cancer. Repairs also have a limit, because getting a home's radon level under 2 pCi/L is hard to do. A smoker whose home is fixed down to that level still lives with the risk that comes with it. Repairs can shrink radon's added danger for that smoker, but they can't erase it. For a smoker, that middle band is better treated as a reason to fix the house than as a reason to wait.

The odds of lung cancer are higher at each step up in radon, so a smoker needs to know exactly where the house sits, not roughly. A test that runs for months gives that answer better than a short one does. A long-term radon test lasts more than 90 days, according to NIH. That is long enough for a smoker to get a steady picture of the home's level before deciding whether to fix it, and how far.

A smoker can't undo the harm of past smoking, but with a careful test, a smoker can learn how much of the danger comes from the house itself.

Many people think of lung cancer as a smoker's disease and assume that a life without cigarettes keeps them clear of it. Radon in the house can still cause the disease in someone who has never lit a cigarette. The EPA estimates that about 7 in 1,000 lifelong nonsmokers could develop lung cancer in homes with radon at 4 pCi/L. Avoiding tobacco does not cancel out the risk from the air in the house. Because that risk comes from the home rather than from a habit, quitting or avoiding anything won't lower it, and the way to reduce it begins with a test of the house.

A never-smoker's odds depend on how much radon is in the air at home. For lifelong nonsmokers in a house at 20 pCi/L, about 36 in 1,000 could get the disease, a far more serious risk than at the lower level. Next-door neighbors with no smoking history may carry very different odds, and the only thing separating them is the radon in each home. For them, the useful question is not whether they are exposed but how much, and only a test of the home can answer it. Nothing about a person shows whether they live with a small risk or a serious one.

Experts disagree over where radon ranks among the causes of lung cancer in lifelong nonsmokers. In the EPA's estimates, radon is the leading cause among non-smokers. The researchers' estimates that the CDC cites put secondhand smoke ahead, with about 7,300 lung cancers a year in never-smokers linked to secondhand smoke and about 2,900 linked to radon. Both sets of estimates count radon as a real cause of the disease in this group, and they differ only on whether it outranks secondhand smoke. Even with those figures putting secondhand smoke ahead, the CDC advises never-smokers to test their homes for radon. Either ranking leads a household to the same step.

For someone who never smoked, the reading in their own house matters more than radon's rank on any national chart.

In 2005, researchers moved the proof of radon's harm out of the mine shaft and into the house. Until then, the case against radon in homes rested on people who worked underground, so its effect in kitchens and bedrooms was an inference drawn from miners. Researchers who studied homes directly had produced mixed results, with some finding a link, some a weak one and some none at all, according to the University of Iowa. The EPA reports that two 2005 studies, one in North America and one in Europe, supplied the direct evidence from homes that earlier work lacked. R. William Field, an associate professor of occupational and environmental health and epidemiology at the University of Iowa, says the European researchers reached the same answer as his North American team, which pooled data from seven home studies. That answer matched the risks the miner studies had predicted, so the danger in houses was no longer a projection.

The 2005 results mattered to homeowners because the risk showed up in ordinary houses rather than only the worst ones. In the North American analysis, prolonged exposure to radon at an average of about 3.0 pCi/L, a level below the EPA's action level, was linked to an 11 to 21 percent rise in lung cancer risk, according to the University of Iowa. That added risk turned up in houses whose owners could have been told no repair was needed. The finding supports the view of Tom Kelly, former director of the EPA's Indoor Environments Division, that even low radon levels can cause the disease. Field says an earlier study of homes in Iowa suggests the true risk from years of breathing radon at home may be slightly higher than the pooled range shows.

A homeowner should read a radon test as a number rather than as a pass or a fail.

By the end of the 1990s, the evidence on radon in homes needed one authoritative account that could support a national death estimate. The National Academy of Sciences published that account in 1999 in its BEIR VI report, which the US EPA calls the most definitive scientific collection on indoor radon. Later work built directly on it. The EPA's later range of radon deaths was the agency's own calculation, and it started from the methods BEIR VI laid out. The newer federal figure therefore grows out of this report's work rather than setting it aside.

The report is usually summed up as a single number, and that number makes it sound more certain than it was. The figure most often tied to BEIR VI is about 15,000 radon deaths a year, yet the US EPA reports that the report's estimate ran from about 15,000 to 22,000. That makes 15,000 the bottom of the report's range, not its final word. The range expresses doubt about how many people radon kills, not about whether it kills them. A later figure above 15,000 does not break with BEIR VI on that fact alone.

That range starts at a yearly toll already in the thousands. Any shift within it leads to more deaths, not fewer. A family waiting for scientists to agree on one number would be waiting on a question about how large the danger is, not whether it exists.

Only a test of the family's own home shows whether its air adds to that toll.

Refining the science on radon made its toll look worse. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency reports that in 2003 it combined the National Academy of Sciences' BEIR VI findings into one model and raised its best estimate of radon deaths to about 21,000 a year. The agency consulted its Science Advisory Board on the revision, so the higher number reflects a scientific review and not only one office's calculation. When the EPA refined the estimate, it ended up with a larger toll. That leaves less room for the hope that earlier fears about radon were overblown.

The 21,000 figure is a best estimate inside a wide range, not the worst case. By the EPA's reckoning, the yearly toll could be as low as 8,000 deaths or as high as 45,000. Even the low end of that range means thousands of Americans dying of lung cancer every year, and no reading of the uncertainty makes radon a minor risk. The doubt in the estimate gives a household a reason to treat the familiar figure as a best guess and no reason to relax.

Federal health agencies give the public the number the EPA calculated in 2003. The CDC repeats the EPA's estimate of yearly U.S. deaths as the national toll on its radon page, which it updated Aug. 26, 2026. The EPA also said it would revise A Citizen's Guide to Radon, the handbook it writes for homeowners, to reflect its new reckoning of the risk. That revision carries the updated science to people deciding whether to test their homes rather than leaving it in a technical report.

The estimate can't show whether any one house adds to that toll. Only a test of that house's air can answer that.

Every official warning about radon ends in the same instruction to households: test your home. The U.S. Surgeon General's warning sets the point for action at 4 pCi/L (150 Bq/m3) or more, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. That number tells a family when to fix the house, not when the air is safe. "There is no known safe level of radon," the CDC said. A reading under 4 means only that the home sits below the level at which the government urges repairs.

The concern reaches far past American homes. As many as 15% of the world's lung cancers may come from radon, the World Health Organization estimated in 2009. That estimate is an upper limit, but even as a limit it makes radon a global health problem rather than a local worry. In many countries, radon in homes accounts for 50% of the natural radiation people are exposed to. The house is a major source of that exposure, and it is also the place where people can find and fix the problem. U.S. and international health officials give the same advice: measure the air where you live, because no reading taken anywhere else can stand in for it.

Radon stays in homes through neglect rather than difficulty. Dr. Michael Repacholi, coordinator of WHO's Radiation and Environmental Health Unit, says the world has paid little attention to a risk that people can lower without much trouble. The fixes are ordinary home repairs, such as sealing cracks and covering the bare earth floors of crawl spaces, according to Department of Housing and Urban Development advice reported by the CDC. The CDC also recommends testing again after repairs, so a household measures whether the work lowered its level instead of assuming it did.

The national and international warnings differ in reach but send a family through the same steps: measure the air, repair the house if the reading calls for it, and measure again.
